2.4
High-accuracy Fourier interpolation

In this section, we introduce how to apply our four-step Fourier set-query framework together with some new techniques to obtain a high-accuracy one-dimensional Fourier interpolation algorithm (Theorem
1.5
), which improves the constant-accuracy algorithm by
[
CKPS16
]
.

Let us briefly summarize the previous algorithm in
[
CKPS16
]
. The high-level idea is to first find some small intervals in the frequency domain such that each contains some significant frequencies of the signal
x^{*}
. (These intervals are called “heavy-clusters” in their paper.) Then, they use some filter techniques (also used in
[
PS15
]
) to reduce the problem of reconstructing
x^{*}
, a signal with multiple heavy-clusters to several single heavy-cluster signals. Then, for each single heavy-cluster signal, since the band-limit is small, they can efficiently estimate its frequencies. Finally, they reconstruct a
\mathrm{poly}(k)
-sparse signal that is close to
x^{*}
via a robust polynomial learning algorithm. More specifically, their algorithm consists of the following steps:

1.

They show that the ground-truth signal
x^{*}(t)=\sum_{j=1}^{k}v_{j}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}t}
can be approximated by
x_{S}(t)=\sum_{j\in S}v_{j}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}t}
, where
S:=\{j\in[k]:f_{j}\in\text{some heavy-cluster}~C_{i}\}
is the set of frequencies in the heavy-clusters. This step will cause an approximation error
E_{1}:=\|x^{*}-x_{S}\|_{T}\leq 1.2{\cal N}
8
8

8

Due to the noisy observations, not every frequency in the heavy-clusters is recoverable. This gap causes an extra implicit error term in
[
CKPS16
]
, which is about
12{\cal N}
. Section
2.4.1
has a more detailed discussion.
, where
{\cal N}^{2}:=\|g\|_{T}^{2}+\delta\|x^{*}\|_{T}^{2}
appears in the approximation error of the Fourier interpolation problem.
However, the frequency in the heavy-cluster can’t be recovered correctly based on the method they proposed. Due to the
S
not having an exact property of one-cluster (See Lemma
12.34
), we will only focus on the frequency with a High signal-noise ratio, which can correctly satisfy the one-cluster property. Furthermore, we define the frequency we focus as

x_{S_{f}}(t)=\sum_{j\in S_{f}}v_{j}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}t}
, where
S_{f}:=\{j\in[k]:f_{j}\in\text{some heavy-cluster}~C_{i}~\text{and}~\text{ bucket}~j~\text{has high signal-noise ratio}\}

2.

They solve a Frequency Estimation problem for
x_{S}
using the filter techniques and multiple-to-one heavy-cluster reduction, and get a list
L
of
O(k)
candidate frequencies so that for each
j\in S
,
f_{j}
is close to some
\widetilde{f}_{p_{j}}\in L
.

3.

The signal
x_{S}(t)
can be decomposed into
\sum_{i=1}^{|L|}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\widetilde{f}_{i}t}\cdot x^{*}_{i}(t)
, where
x^{*}_{i}(t):=\sum_{j:p_{j}=i}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}(f_{j}-\widetilde{f}_{i})}
is a one-cluster signal with small band-limit. For each
x^{*}_{i}(t)
, they prove that there exists a low-degree polynomial
P_{i}(t)
that can approximate it. Let’s denote the polynomial-approximated signal
\sum_{i=1}^{|L|}e^{2\pi\widetilde{f}_{i}t}\cdot P_{i}(t)
by
x_{S,\mathsf{poly}}(t)
, which has an approximation error
E_{2}:=\|x_{S}-x_{S,\mathsf{poly}}\|_{T}\leq\delta\|x_{S}\|_{T}
.
9
9

9

We remark that even if the ground-truth signal
x^{*}(t)
can be well-approximated by a mixed Fourier-polynomial signal
\widetilde{x}(t)
, we are unable to recover
every
basis of
\widetilde{x}(t)
due to the limitation of the frequency estimation procedure. Thus, directly applying linear regression to
partially
reconstruct
\widetilde{x}(t)
will not guarantee to be a
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximation of
x^{*}(t)
.

4.

It remains to reconstruct
x_{S,\mathsf{poly}}(t)
, which is a variant of Signal Estimation problem. They use a sampling-and-regression approach to obtain a
\mathrm{poly}(k)
-Fourier-sparse signal
y(t)
with an approximation error
E_{3}:=\|y-x_{S,\mathsf{poly}}\|_{T}\leq 2200{\cal N}
.

By triangle inequality, the total approximation error is
\|y-x^{*}\|_{T}\leq E_{1}+E_{2}+E_{3}\leq C\cdot{\cal N}
, where
C>1000
is an absolute constant.

However, the frequency in the heavy-cluster can’t be recovered correctly based on the method they proposed. Due to the
S
not having an exact property of a one-cluster (See Lemma
12.34
), we will only focus on the frequency with a high signal-to-noise ratio that can correctly satisfy the one-cluster property.
Furthermore, we will focus on

x_{S_{f}}(t)=\sum_{j\in S_{f}}v_{j}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}t},

where
S_{f}:=\{j\in[k]:f_{j}\in\text{some heavy-cluster}~C_{i}~\text{and the bucket}~j~\text{has a high signal-to-noise ratio}\}
.
In the remainder of this section, we first introduce our techniques to achieve a
(9+\varepsilon)
-approximation error. Then, we show how to overcome the barrier and achieve a
(3+\sqrt{2}+\varepsilon)
-approximation error.

2.4.1
(9+\varepsilon)
-approximation algorithm

High sensitivity frequency estimation

We first improve
E_{1}
from
1.2{\cal N}
to
(1+\varepsilon){\cal N}
by proposing a high sensitivity frequency estimation method. More specifically, to identify the heavy-clusters of the signal
x^{*}
,
[
CKPS16
]
designed a filter function
H
such that
H\cdot x^{*}
has high energy in each heavy cluster
C_{i}
; that is,

\displaystyle\int_{C_{i}}|\widehat{H\cdot x^{*}}(f)|^{2}\mathrm{d}f\geq\frac{T}{k}{\cal N}^{2}.

(2)

Moreover,
H
’s frequencies are contained in a small interval of length
\Delta
. These two properties imply that for any true frequency
f_{i}\in C_{i}
, the signal
H\cdot x^{*}
with frequency domain restricted to
[f_{i}-\Delta,f_{i}+\Delta]
is a one heavy-cluster signal with small band-limit, which allows us to use
[
PS15
]
’s approach to estimate
f_{i}
. The filter function
H
in
[
CKPS16
]
is only
O(1)
-sensitive, which means it can concentrate a constant fraction of the signal’s energy. And for those less important frequencies, they cannot be clustered by
H
and will be lost in the frequency estimation procedure.

We manage to modify their filter construction and obtain a
(1-\varepsilon)
-sensitive filter function
H
such that the signal
x_{S^{*}}
consisted of the frequencies in the new heavy-clusters has about
(1-\varepsilon)
-fraction of energy of
x^{*}
. More specifically, we have
E^{\mathsf{new}}_{1}=\|x^{*}-x_{S^{*}}\|_{T}\leq(1+\varepsilon){\cal N}
.

To prove that we can actually estimate the frequencies in the new heavy-clusters, we observe a subtle point: the energy condition of heavy-cluster and the energy condition of frequency estimation are inconsistent due to the
noise
in observations. To be able to estimate the one heavy-cluster signal’s frequency, it is required that

\displaystyle\int_{C_{i}}|\widehat{H\cdot x}(f)|^{2}\mathrm{d}f\geq\frac{T}{k}{\cal N}^{2},

(3)

which is different from Eq. (
2
).
In other words, not all frequencies in
S^{*}
are recoverable, but only most of them. Since
[
CKPS16
]
only wants a constant approximation error, they may simply ignore this difference by losing a constant factor in accuracy. For us, however, we need to make it precise. We define
S
to be a subset of
S^{*}
containing the frequencies in the heavy-clusters satisfying Eq. (
3
). We analyze the effect of
H\cdot g
and show that by strengthening the RHS of heavy-cluster’s energy condition (Eq. (
2
)) to
\frac{4T}{k}{\cal N}^{2}
, we can bound the unrecoverable part’s energy by

E_{1.5}:=\|x_{S^{*}}-x_{S}\|_{T}\leq(2+\varepsilon){\cal N}.

Given the definition of
S_{f}
as provided earlier, we also need to establish the upper bound of

\displaystyle E_{S_{f}}:=\|x_{S_{f}}-x_{S}\|_{T}\leq(1+\varepsilon)\mathcal{N}.

For the recoverable part
x_{S_{f}}
, we can just follow
[
CKPS16
]
’s approach to estimating the frequencies in each heavy-cluster.

Generalized high-accuracy signal estimation

We apply our four-step Fourier set-query framework to solve the Signal Estimation problem in the three-step of
[
CKPS16
]
’s algorithm and improve
E_{3}
from
2200{\cal N}
to
(4+\varepsilon){\cal N}
. We first define the problem more formally. By frequency estimation, we obtain a list of candidate frequencies of
x_{S}
and in the third step, we know that it can be approximated by
x_{S_{f},\mathsf{poly}}(t):=\sum_{i=1}^{|L|}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\widetilde{f}_{i}t}\cdot P_{i}(t)
with very tiny error
E_{2}
, where
P_{i}(t)
are some degree-
d
polynomials. We can rewrite
x_{S,\mathsf{poly}}
in the
Fourier-monomial mixed basis
:

\displaystyle x_{S,\mathsf{poly}}(t)=\sum_{i=1}^{|L|}\sum_{j=0}^{d}v_{i,j}\cdot e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\widetilde{f}_{i}t}t^{j},

where
v_{i,j}\in\mathbb{C}
and
\widetilde{f}_{i}\in L
are known. That is, we need to learn
\{v_{i,j}\}
given noisy observations
x_{S_{f},\mathsf{poly}}(t)+g^{\prime}(t)
, which is a Signal Estimation problem for the following family of signals:

\displaystyle{\cal F}_{\mathsf{mix}}:=\mathrm{span}\left\{e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\widetilde{f}_{i}t}\cdot t^{j}~\bigg|~i\in[|L|],j\in\{0,\cdots,d\}\right\}.

We apply our three-step framework as follows. In Step 1, we need an energy bound for
{\cal F}_{\mathsf{mix}}
.
[
CKPS16
]
showed that
R_{\mathsf{mix}}:=\sup_{u(t)\in{\cal F}_{\mathsf{mix}}}\frac{\sup_{t}|u(t)|^{2}}{\|u\|_{T}^{2}}\leq\widetilde{O}(|L|^{4}d^{4})
. Then, we get that uniformly sample
\widetilde{O}(|L|^{4}d^{4}\varepsilon^{-1})
points in
[0,T]
gives an oblivious sketching for
x_{S,\mathsf{poly}}
. Furthermore, we can show that this sampler is
\varepsilon
-well-balanced. In Step 2, since we aim at achieving high accuracy, we do not distill the sketch but directly apply the sharper error analysis to control the energy of the orthogonal part of the noise, as we did for our high-accuracy signal estimation algorithm (Theorem
1.1
). Finally, in Step 3, we solve a weighted linear regression to estimate the coefficients and obtain a signal
y^{\prime}(t)\in{\cal F}_{\mathsf{mix}}
such that

\displaystyle E^{\mathsf{new}}_{3}:=\|y^{\prime}-x_{S_{f},\mathsf{poly}}\|_{T}\leq(1+\varepsilon)\|x-x_{S_{f},\mathsf{poly}}\|_{T}\leq(4+\varepsilon){\cal N}.

Then, we can transform
y
back to a
\mathrm{poly}(k)
-Fourier-sparse signal with error
\|y-y^{\prime}\|_{T}\leq E_{2}
.

Combining them together and re-scaling
\varepsilon
and
\delta
, we get that

\displaystyle\|y-x^{*}\|_{T}\leq

\displaystyle~\|y-y^{\prime}\|_{T}+\|y-x_{S_{f},\mathsf{poly}}\|_{T}+\|x_{S_{f},\mathsf{poly}}-x_{S_{f}}\|_{T}+\|x_{S_{f}}-x_{S}\|_{T}+\|x_{S}-x_{S^{*}}\|_{T}+\|x_{S^{*}}-x^{*}\|_{T}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~E_{2}+E_{3}^{\mathsf{new}}+E_{2}+E_{S_{f}}+E_{1.5}+E_{1}^{\mathsf{new}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~(9+\varepsilon)\|g\|_{T}+\delta\|x^{*}\|_{T}.

Therefore, we obtain a Fourier interpolation algorithm with
(9+\varepsilon)
-approximation error.

2.4.2
(3+\sqrt{2}+\varepsilon)
-approximation algorithm

How can we further improve this algorithm? We observe that
E^{\mathsf{new}}_{3}
can be written more precisely as
(1+\varepsilon)\|g\|_{T}+(3+\varepsilon){\cal N}
, where the first term
(1+\varepsilon)\|g\|_{T}
is identical to the Signal Estimation problem’s approximation error (Theorem
1.1
) and may not be further improved. On the other hand,
E_{1}^{\mathsf{new}}
and
E_{1.5}
only depend on
{\cal N}
. If we can take a smaller value for
{\cal N}^{2}
, i.e.,
\varepsilon(\|g\|_{T}^{2}+\delta\|x\|_{T}^{2})
, then we will improve approximation error. We show that it is possible via an
ultra-high sensitivity frequency estimation method
.

Ultra-high sensitivity frequency estimation

To improve the sensitivity of the frequency estimation method, let
{\cal N}_{1}^{2}:=\varepsilon{\cal N}^{2}
and consider the heavy-clusters with parameter
{\cal N}_{1}
. Let
S^{*}_{1}
denote the set of frequencies of
x^{*}(t)
in the
{\cal N}_{1}
-heavy-clusters. By the same analysis as in our previous frequency estimation approach, we have
E_{1}^{\mathsf{new}+}:=\|x^{*}-x_{S_{1}^{*}}\|_{T}\leq(1+\varepsilon){\cal N}_{1}
.

However, due to the inconsistent energy conditions, only those frequencies in the heavy-clusters satisfying Eq. (
3
) are recoverable. Let
S_{1}
denote the set of such frequencies, and we need to upper bound
\|x_{S_{1}^{*}}-x_{S_{1}}\|_{T}
. Previously, we strengthen the heavy-cluster’s condition (Eq. (
2
)) and get a
E_{1.5}\leq(2+\varepsilon)\|g\|_{T}
bound. Here, instead, we relax the RHS of Eq. (
3
) to
\varepsilon\cdot\frac{T}{k}{\cal N}_{1}^{2}
. Intuitively, more frequencies will satisfy the new frequency estimation condition; and if there is a unrecoverable frequency
f^{*}\in S^{*}_{1}\backslash S_{1}
, it indicates that its contribution in filtered signal
H\cdot x^{*}
is cancelled out by the filtered noise
H\cdot g
.
Using this
signal-noise cancellation effect
, we prove that:

\displaystyle\|H(x_{S_{1}}-x_{S^{*}_{1}})\|_{T}^{2}+\|H(x-x_{S_{1}})\|_{T}^{2}\leq(1+\sqrt{\varepsilon})\|x-x_{S^{*}_{1}}\|_{T}^{2},

(4)

which saves a factor of 2 from
E_{1.5}
by introducing an extra term
\|H(x-x_{S_{1}})\|_{T}
. Recall
\|x-x_{S_{1}}\|_{T}
is related to
E_{3}^{\mathsf{new}}
, the error of the signal estimation procedure. We can decompose it into the “passing energy”
\|H(x-x_{S_{1}})\|_{T}
and “filtered energy”
\|(\mathrm{Id}-H)(x-x_{S_{1}})\|_{T}
and bound them by:

\displaystyle\|x-x_{S_{1}}\|_{T}\leq\|H(x-x_{S_{1}})\|_{T}+\|g\|_{T}+O(\varepsilon)\|x^{*}-x_{S_{1}}\|_{T}.

Thus, Eq. (
4
) can be considered as upper-bounding
E_{1.5}
and
E_{3}^{\mathsf{new}}
simultaneously. Combining them together, we get the following error guarantee for the frequency recoverable signal
x_{S_{1}}
:

\displaystyle\|x-x_{S_{1}}\|_{T}+\|x^{*}-x_{S_{1}}\|_{T}\leq(1+\sqrt{2}+O(\sqrt{\varepsilon}))\|g\|_{T}+O(\sqrt{\delta})\|x^{*}\|_{T}.

(5)

(a)
\|H(t)\cdot(x_{S^{*}_{1}}-x_{S_{1}})(t)\|_{T}

(b)
\|H(t)\cdot(x-x_{S_{1}})(t)\|_{T}

Figure 4:

An illustration of the signal-noise cancellation effect (Eq. (
4
)). In (a), the blue region corresponds to the first term of Eq. (
4
), which roughly equals the energy of
x-x_{S^{*}_{1}}
. In (b), the red and blue regions correspond to the second term, where most of the energy is canceled. Thus, their total energy is very close to
\|x-x_{S^{*}_{1}}\|_{T}
.

Then, by a more careful analysis of the
HashToBins
approach used by
[
CKPS16
]
for Frequency Estimation, we show that
x_{S_{1}}
’s frequencies can be efficiently approximated, which gives an ultra-high sensitivity frequency estimation method.

The remaining part of the algorithm is almost identical to the previous one. We run the high-accuracy signal estimation algorithm to reconstruct
x_{S_{1}}
. Let
y(t)
denote the output Fourier-sparse signal. By Eq. (
5
) and re-scaling
\varepsilon
and
\delta
, we have

\displaystyle\|y-x^{*}\|_{T}\leq(3+\sqrt{2}+\varepsilon)\|g\|_{T}+\delta\|x^{*}\|_{T}.

Therefore, we achieve a
(3+\sqrt{2}+\varepsilon)
-approximation error for the Fourier interpolation.

Paper Organization

The remainder of the paper is organized as follows.
In Sections
3
, we formally define and study the “semi-continuous” Fourier interpolation and set query problems over lattices. In Section
4
, we provide some preliminaries on Fourier transformation, lattices, etc.

Then, we focus on developing Fourier set query algorithms based on our three-step framework (Section
2.1
). We first build some technical components in Sections
5
-
8
.
Section
5
is for Step 1, where we prove energy bounds and concentration properties for high-dimensional Fourier-sparse signals and discrete Fourier-sparse signals. Section
6
is for Step 2, where we describe fast
oblivious
sketching methods and we use them as “preconditioners” for continuous and discrete Fourier-sparse signals in one and higher dimensions.
Sections
7
and
8
are for Step 3. More specifically, in Sections
7
, we design a data structure for improving the time/space complexity of the Randomized BSS algorithm (Theorem
2.1
). Using this data structure, in Section
8
we describe the
sketch distillation
technique for different kinds of Fourier-sparse signals and analyze its robustness to noise. Finally, these components are wrapped up in Sections
9
-
11
. Section
9
shows sample-optimal and high-accuracy signal estimation algorithms for one-dimensional semi-continuous signals (Theorem
1.1
). Section
10
generalizes to high-dimensional signals using lattice theory. Section
11
gives a discrete Fourier set query algorithm (Theorem
1.2
).

In the last part of paper, in Section
12
, we give a high-accuracy Fourier interpolation algorithm for one-dimensional continuous signals, based on our sharper error control and efficient Fourier set query algorithm.

In the appendix, Section
A
discusses a special case of the Signal Estimation problem where the observation has no noise. We present a straightforward algorithm for one-dimensional Fourier sparse signals. Section
B
proves that any signal can be approximated by a semi-continuous signal with the same sparsity and polynomially-small frequency gap
10
10

10

We also show an approximation with
O(1)
-frequency gap but slightly worse sparsity.
, which implies a Fourier interpolation algorithm with optimal output-sparsity with a different error guarantee. Finally, Section
C
shows that the accuracy of our Fourier interpolation algorithm can be further improved, if we only care about the signal in a subset of time duration.

Notations.

For any positive integer
n
, we use
[n]
to denote
\{1,2,\cdots,n\}
. We use
\mathbf{i}
to denote
\sqrt{-1}
. For a complex number
z\in\mathbb{C}
where
z=a+\mathbf{i}b
and
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
. We use
\overline{z}
to denote the complex conjugate of
z
, i.e.,
\overline{z}=a-\mathbf{i}b
. Then it is obvious that
|z|^{2}=z\cdot\overline{z}=a^{2}+b^{2}
. We use
f\lesssim g
to denote that there exists a constant
C
such that
f\leq Cg
, and
f\eqsim g
to denote
f\lesssim g\lesssim f
. We use
\widetilde{O}(f)
to denote
f\log^{O(1)}(f)
.
We say
x(t)
is a
k
-Fourier-sparse when
x(t)=\sum_{j=1}^{k}v_{j}\exp(2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}t)
. We use
\widehat{x}(f)
to denote the Fourier transform of
x(t)
. More specifically,
\widehat{x}(f)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}x(t)\exp(-2\pi\mathbf{i}ft)\mathrm{d}t
.
We define our discrete norm as
\|g(t)\|^{2}_{W}=\frac{1}{|W|}\sum_{t\in W}|g(t)|^{2}
for function
g
. We define our weighted discrete norm as
\|g(t)\|^{2}_{S,w}=\sum_{t\in S}w_{t}|g(t)|^{2}
for function
g
. We define the continuous
T
-norm as
\|g(t)\|^{2}_{T}=\frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^{T}|g(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t
for function
g
.

In general, we assume
x^{*}(t)
is our ground truth and is a
k
-Fourier-sparse signal. We can observe function
x(t)=x^{*}(t)+g(t)
for
g(t)
being a noise function. We can observe
x(t)
in duration
[0,T]
. The ground truth
x^{*}(t)
has frequencies in
[-F,F]
.

3
Definitions of Semi-Continuous Fourier Set Query and Interpolation

In this section, we give the formal definitions of the problems studied in this paper. In Section
3.1
, we define the Fourier set query for discrete and continuous signals. In Section
3.2
, we define the Fourier interpolation problem and its two sub-problems: frequency estimation and signal estimation.

3.1
Formal definitions of Fourier set query

The discrete Fourier set query problem is defined as follows:

Definition 3.1

(Discrete Fourier set query problem)
.

Let
x\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
and
\widehat{x}
be its discrete Fourier transformation. Let
\varepsilon>0
. Given a set
S\subseteq[n]
and query access to
x
, the goal is to use a few queries to compute a vector
x^{\prime}
with support
\mathrm{supp}(x^{\prime})\subseteq S
such that

\displaystyle\|(x^{\prime}-\widehat{x})_{S}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\varepsilon\cdot\|\widehat{x}_{[n]\backslash S}\|_{2}^{2}.

We also define the continuous Fourier set query problem as follows:

Definition 3.2

(Continuous Fourier set query problem)
.

For
d\geq 1
, let
x^{*}(t)
be a signal in time duration
[0,T]^{d}
. Let
\widehat{x^{*}}(f)
denote the continuous Fourier transformation of
x^{*}(t)
. Let
\varepsilon>0
. Given a set
S\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
of frequencies such that
\mathrm{supp}(\widehat{x^{*}})\subseteq S
, and observations of the form
x(t)=x^{*}(t)+g(t)
, where
g(t)
denotes the noise. The goal is to output a Fourier-sparse signal
x^{\prime}(t)
with support
\mathrm{supp}(\widehat{x^{\prime}})\subseteq S
such that

\displaystyle\|x^{\prime}-x^{*}\|_{T}^{2}\leq(1+\varepsilon)\cdot\|g\|_{T}^{2}.

3.2
Formal definitions of semi-continuous Fourier interpolation

In this section, we provide the following formal definition of the semi-continuous Fourier interpolation problem, where we assume that the frequencies of the signal are contained in a lattice.

Problem 3.3

(Semi-continuous Fourier interpolation problem)
.

Given a basis
\mathcal{B}
of
m
known vectors
b_{1},b_{2},\cdots b_{m}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, let
\Lambda(\mathcal{B})\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
denote the lattice

\displaystyle\Lambda(\mathcal{B})=\Big\{z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:z=\sum_{i=1}^{m}c_{i}b_{i},c_{i}\in\mathbb{Z},\forall i\in[m]\Big\}

Suppose that
f_{1},f_{2},\cdots,f_{k}\in\Lambda(\mathcal{B})
,
\forall i\in[k],|f_{i}|\leq F
. Let
x^{*}(t)=\sum_{j=1}^{k}v_{j}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\langle f_{j},t\rangle}
, and let
g(t)
denote the noise. Given observations of the form
x(t)=x^{*}(t)+g(t)
,
t\in[0,T]^{d}
. Let
\eta=\min_{i\neq j}\|f_{j}-f_{i}\|_{\infty}
. There are three goals:

1.

The first goal is to design an algorithm that output
f_{1},f_{2},\cdots,f_{k}

exactly
given query access to the signal
x(t)
for
t\in[0,T]^{d}
.

2.

The second goal is to design an algorithm that output a set
L
of frequencies such that, for each
f_{i}
, there is
f^{\prime}_{i}\in L
,
\|f_{i}-f^{\prime}_{i}\|_{2}\leq D/T
.

3.

The third goal is to design an algorithm that output
y(t)=\sum_{j=1}^{\widetilde{k}}v_{j}^{\prime}\cdot e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}^{\prime}t}
such that
\int_{[0,T]^{d}}|y(t)-x(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t\lesssim\int_{[0,T]^{d}}|g(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t
.

Then, we extract two sub-problems from Problem
3.3
: Frequency Estimation and Signal Estimation. We give their definitions below.

We first define the
d
-dimensional frequency estimation under the semi-continuous as follows. In this problem, we want to recover each frequencies in a small range.

Problem 3.4

(Frequency estimation)
.

Given a basis
\mathcal{B}
of
m
known vectors
b_{1},b_{2},\cdots b_{m}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, let
\Lambda(\mathcal{B})\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
denote the lattice

\displaystyle\Lambda(\mathcal{B})=\Big\{z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:z=\sum_{i=1}^{m}c_{i}b_{i},c_{i}\in\mathbb{Z},\forall i\in[m]\Big\}

Suppose that
f_{1},f_{2},\cdots,f_{k}\in\Lambda(\mathcal{B})
. Let
x^{*}(t)=\sum_{j=1}^{k}v_{j}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\langle f_{j},t\rangle}
,
and let
g(t)
denote the noise. Given observations of the form
x(t)=x^{*}(t)+g(t)
,
t\in[0,T]^{d}
. Let
\eta=\min_{i\neq j}\|f_{j}-f_{i}\|_{\infty}
.

The goal is to design an algorithm that output a set
L
of frequencies such that, for each
f_{i}
, there is
f^{\prime}_{i}\in L
,
\|f_{i}-f^{\prime}_{i}\|_{2}\leq D/T
.

We remark that the recovered frequencies in
L
are not necessary to be in
\Lambda(\mathcal{B})
, and
D
is a parameter that can depend on
k
.

Next, we define the
d
-dimensional Signal Estimation under the semi-continuous setting as follows. In this problem, we want to recover a signal that can approximate the ground-truth signal in the time domain.

Problem 3.5

(Signal Estimation problem)
.

Given a basis
\mathcal{B}
of
m
known vectors
b_{1},b_{2},\cdots b_{m}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, let
\Lambda(\mathcal{B})\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
denote the lattice

\displaystyle\Lambda(\mathcal{B})=\Big\{z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:z=\sum_{i=1}^{m}c_{i}b_{i},c_{i}\in\mathbb{Z},\forall i\in[m]\Big\}

Suppose that
f_{1},f_{2},\cdots,f_{k}\in\Lambda(\mathcal{B})
. Let
x^{*}(t):=\sum_{j=1}^{k}v_{j}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\langle f_{j},t\rangle}
, and let
g(t)
denote the noise. Given observations of the form
x(t)=x^{*}(t)+g(t)
,
t\in[0,T]^{d}
. Let
\eta=\min_{i\neq j}\|f_{j}-f_{i}\|_{\infty}
.

The goal is to design an algorithm that outputs
y(t)=\sum_{j=1}^{\widetilde{k}}v_{j}^{\prime}\cdot e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}^{\prime}t}
such that

\int_{[0,T]^{d}}|y(t)-x(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t\lesssim\int_{[0,T]^{d}}|g(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t.

Note that outputting
y(t)=\sum_{j=1}^{\widetilde{k}}v_{j}^{\prime}\cdot e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}^{\prime}t}
means outputting
\{v_{j}^{\prime},f_{j}^{\prime}\}_{j\in[\widetilde{k}]}
.

Remark 3.6
.

We note that given the solution of Frequency Estimation (Problem
3.4
), Signal Estimation (Problem
3.5
) can be formulated as a Fourier set query problem (Problem
3.2
). More specifically, by Frequency Estimation, we will find a set that contains all frequencies of the ground truth signal
x^{*}(t)
. Then, we only need to recover the coefficients with frequencies in this set, which is equivalent to a set query problem.

4
Preliminaries

This section is organized as follows. In Section
4.1
, we provide some technical tools in probability theory and linear algebra. In Section
4.2
, we review the Fourier transformation for different types of signals. In Section
4.3
, we show some facts about Lattices. And in Section
4.4
, we discuss the importance sampling method.

4.1
Tools and inequalities

Lemma 4.1

(Chernoff Bound
[
Che52
]
)
.

Let
X_{1},X_{2},\cdots,X_{n}
be independent random variables. Assume that
0\leq X_{i}\leq 1
always, for each
i\in[n]
. Let
X=X_{1}+X_{2}+\cdots+X_{n}
and
\mu=\mathbb{E}[X]=\overset{n}{\underset{i=1}{\sum}}\mathbb{E}[X_{i}]
. Then for any
\varepsilon>0
,

\mathsf{Pr}[X\geq(1+\varepsilon)\mu]\leq\exp(-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2+\varepsilon}\mu)\textit{ and }\mathsf{Pr}[X\leq(1-\varepsilon)\mu]\leq\exp(-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2}\mu).

Definition 4.2

(
\varepsilon
-net)
.

Let
T
be a metric space with distance measure
d
. Consider a subset
K\subset T
and let
\varepsilon>0
. A subset
{\cal N}\subseteq K
is called an
\varepsilon
-net of
K
if every point in
K
is within distance
\varepsilon
of some point of
{\cal N}
, i.e.

\displaystyle\forall x\in K,\exists y\in{\cal N}~\text{s.t.}~d(x,y)\leq\varepsilon.

Fact 4.3

(Fast matrix multiplication)
.

We use
{\cal T}_{\mathrm{mat}}(a,b,c)
to denote the time of multiplying an
a\times b
matrix with another
b\times c
matrix.

We use
\omega
to denote the exponent of matrix multiplication, i.e.,
{\cal T}_{\mathrm{mat}}(n,n,n)=n^{\omega}
. Currently
\omega\approx 2.373

[
Wil12
,
LG14
,
AW21
]
.

Fact 4.4

(Weighted linear regression)
.

Given a matrix
A\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times d}
, a vector
b\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
and a weight vector
w\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{n}
, it takes
O(nd^{\omega-1})
time to output an
x^{\prime}
such that

x^{\prime}=\underset{x}{\arg\min}\|\sqrt{W}(Ax-b)\|_{2}=(A^{*}WA)^{-1}A^{*}Wb.

where
\sqrt{W}:=\mathrm{diag}(\sqrt{w_{1}},\dots,\sqrt{w_{n}})\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
, and
\omega\approx 2.373
is the exponent of matrix multiplication
[
Wil12
,
LG14
,
AW21
]
.

Fact 4.5
.

For any
x\in(0,1)
, we have
\cos(x)\leq\exp(-x^{2}/2)
.

4.2
Basics of Fourier transformation

The definition of high dimensional Fourier transform is as follows:

\displaystyle\widehat{x}(f)=\int_{(-\infty,\infty)^{d}}{x}(t)\exp(-2\pi\mathbf{i}\langle f,t\rangle)\mathrm{d}t,\text{~where~}f\in\mathbb{R}^{d},

and the definition of high dimensional inverse Fourier transform is as follows:

\displaystyle x(t)=\int_{(-\infty,\infty)^{d}}\widehat{x}(f)\exp(2\pi\mathbf{i}\langle f,t\rangle)\mathrm{d}f,\text{~where~}t\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

Note that when we replace
d=1
in the definition of high dimensional Fourier transform and inverse Fourier transform above, we get the definition of one-dimensional Fourier transform and inverse Fourier transform.

The definition of discrete Fourier transform is as follows:

\displaystyle\widehat{x}_{f}=\sum_{t=1}^{n}{x}_{t}\exp(-2\pi\mathbf{i}ft/n),\text{~where~}f\in[n],

and the definition of discrete inverse Fourier transform is as follows:

\displaystyle x_{t}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{f=1}^{n}\widehat{x}_{f}\exp(2\pi\mathbf{i}ft/n),\text{~where~}t\in[n].

A continuous
k
-Fourier sparse signal
x(t):\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
can be represented as follows:

\displaystyle x(t)=\sum_{j=1}^{k}v_{j}\exp(2\pi\mathbf{i}\langle f_{j},t\rangle),~v_{j}\in\mathbb{C},f_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},~\forall j\in[k].

Thus,
\widehat{x}(f)
is:

\displaystyle\widehat{x}(f)=\sum_{j=1}^{k}v_{j}\delta(t-f_{j}).

A discrete
k
-Fourier sparse signal
x\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
can be represented as follows:

\displaystyle x_{t}=\sum_{j\in S}v_{j}\exp(2\pi\mathbf{i}jt/n),~S\subseteq[n],|S|=k,~v_{j}\in\mathbb{C},\forall j\in S.

So,
\widehat{x}_{f}
is:

\displaystyle\widehat{x}_{f}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}v_{j}&,j\in S\\
0&,\text{o.w.}\end{array}\right.

4.3
Facts about Lattices

Definition 4.6

(Lattice)
.

A lattice
{\cal L}
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
is defined as follows:

\displaystyle{\cal L}:=\Big\{\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lambda_{i}b_{i}:\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{k}\in\mathbb{Z}\Big\},

where
b_{1},\dots,b_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
are linearly independent vectors. And we denote the matrix
B:=\begin{bmatrix}b_{1}&\cdots&b_{k}\end{bmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
as the basis of the lattice
{\cal L}
.

Definition 4.7

(Fundamental parallelepiped)
.

For a lattice
{\cal L}
with basis
B
, its fundamental parallelepiped is defined to be:

\displaystyle{\cal P}(B):=\{Bx~|~x\in[0,1)^{d}\}.

Fact 4.8
.

For any lattice with basis
B
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{vol}({\cal P}(B))=\sqrt{\det(B^{\top}B)}.

In particular, if
B
is full-rank,
\mathrm{vol}({\cal P}(B))=|\det(B)|
.

Lemma 4.9

(The number of lattice points within a ball
11
11

11

We thank Thomas Rothvoss for providing the proof of this bound.
)
.

Let
{\cal L}
be any lattice with basis
B
such that the spectral norm
\|B\|\leq\ell
.
Then, the number of lattice points inside a ball centered at 0 with radius
R
is upper bounded by:

\displaystyle|{\cal L}\cap B_{d}(0,R)|\leq(1+\frac{\sqrt{k}\ell}{R})^{k}\cdot\frac{\mathrm{vol}(B_{k}(0,R))}{\mathrm{vol}({\cal P}(B))}.

Proof.

We first show that for two different lattice points
x,y\in{\cal L}\cap B_{d}(0,R)
, the translations of
{\cal P}
at
x
and at
y
are disjoint, i.e.,
(x+{\cal P})\cap(y+{\cal P})=\emptyset
.

Suppose
(x+{\cal P})\cap(y+{\cal P})\neq\emptyset
for some
x,y\in{\cal L}\cap B_{d}(0,R)
. Then, we have

\displaystyle x+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lambda_{i}b_{i}=y+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\tau_{i}b_{i},

where
\lambda_{i},\tau_{i}\in[0,1)
. It gives

\displaystyle x-y=\sum_{i=1}^{k}(\tau_{i}-\lambda_{i})b_{i}.

Note that
x-y\in{\cal L}
, which means

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{k}(\tau_{i}-\lambda_{i})b_{i}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}c_{i}b_{i},

where
c_{1},\dots,c_{k}\in\mathbb{Z}
. Since
\tau_{i}-\lambda_{i}\in(-1,1)
, we get that
c_{i}=0
for all
i\in[k]
. Thus,
x=y
.

Then, for any point
y\in x+{\cal P}
, where
x\in{\cal L}\cap B_{d}(0,R)
, we have

\displaystyle\|y\|_{2}=\|x+z\|_{2}\leq\|x\|_{2}+\|z\|_{2}\leq R+\sqrt{k}\ell,

(6)

where
z\in{\cal P}
and the last step follows from
x\in B_{d}(0,R)
and
\|z\|_{2}=\|B\lambda\|_{2}\leq\|B\|\|\lambda\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{k}\ell
, for some
\lambda\in[0,1)^{k}
.

Then, we have

\displaystyle|{\cal L}\cap B_{d}(0,R)|\leq

\displaystyle~\sum_{x\in{\cal L}\cap B(0,R)}\frac{\mathrm{vol}(x+{\cal P})}{\mathrm{vol}({\cal P}(B))}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\frac{\mathrm{vol}(B_{k}(0,R+\sqrt{k}\ell))}{\mathrm{vol}({\cal P}(B))}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~(1+\frac{\sqrt{k}\ell}{R})^{k}\cdot\frac{\mathrm{vol}(B_{k}(0,R))}{\mathrm{vol}({\cal P}(B))},

where the second step follows from the disjointness of translations and the bound on the total width (Eq. (
6
)).

The lemma is then proved.
∎

We define the shortest vector problem (SVP) as follows:

Definition 4.10

(SVP)
.

Let
{\cal L}
denote a Lattice.
We define
\mathrm{SVP}({\cal L})
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{SVP}({\cal L}):=\min\{\|x\|_{2}~|~x\in{\cal L}\backslash\{{\bf 0}\}\}.

Given a basis of
{\cal L}
, the goal is to compute
\mathrm{SVP}({\cal L})
.

In fact, compute SVP (or even approximations of SVP) is an NP-hard problem.
The following theorem shows a well-known lower bound for the shortest vector length.

Theorem 4.11

(Theorem 1.10 in
[
Rot16
]
, a lower bound on shortest vector)
.

Let
{\cal L}
denote a lattice with basis
{\cal B}
. Let
(b_{1}^{*},\cdots,b_{n}^{*})
be its Gram-Schmit orthogonalization. Then

\displaystyle\mathrm{SVP}({\cal L})\geq\min_{i\in[n]}\|b_{i}^{*}\|_{2}

Fact 4.12
.

The Gram–Schmidt process takes a finite, linearly independent set of vectors
S=\{v_{1},\cdots,v_{k}\}
for
k\leq n
, runs
O(nk^{2})
time, and generates an orthogonal set
S^{\prime}=\{u_{1},\cdots,u_{k}\}
that spans the same
k
-dimensional subspace of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
as
S
.

4.4
Facts about importance sampling

Important sampling try to estimate a statistic value in one distribution by taking samples in another distribution. In particular,
[
CP19a
]
considered the importance sampling for estimating the norm of functions in a linear family
{\cal F}
.

In this followings, we first provide some basic definitions about linear function family.

Definition 4.13

(Condition number of sampling distribution)
.

Let
G
be any domain and
{\cal F}
is a linear function family from
G
to
\mathbb{C}
. Let
D
be an arbitrary distribution over
G
. Then the condition number of
D
with respect to
{\cal F}
is defined as follows:

K_{D}:=\sup_{t\in G}\sup_{f\in{\mathcal{F}}}\frac{|f(t)|^{2}}{\|f\|_{D}^{2}},

where

\displaystyle\|f\|_{D}^{2}:=\int_{G}D(t)\cdot|f(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t.

Definition 4.14

(Orthonormal basis for linear function family)
.

Let
G
be any domain. Given a linear function family
\mathcal{F}
from
G
to
C
, and a probability distribution
D
over
G
. We say
\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{d}\}
form an orthonormal basis of
\mathcal{F}
with respect to
D
, if they satisfy the following properties:

•

for any
i,j\in[d]
,
\int_{G}D(t)v_{i}(t)\overline{v_{j}(t)}\mathrm{d}t={\bf 1}_{i=j}
, and

•

for any
f\in{\cal F}
,
f\in\mathrm{span}\{v_{1},\dots,v_{d}\}
.

Fact 4.15
.

Let
\{v_{1},\dots,v_{k}\}
be an orthonormal basis of
{\cal F}
with respect to
D
. For any function
f\in\mathcal{F}
, let
\alpha(f)
denote the coefficients under the basis
\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{d}\}
, i.e.,
h=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\alpha(h)_{i}\cdot v_{i}
. Then,

\|\alpha(h)\|_{2}=\|h\|_{D}.

For an unknown function
f\in{\cal F}
, the goal of importance sampling is to estimate
\|f\|_{D}
, given samples from another distribution
D^{\prime}
. The following definition introduces the importance sampling procedure and condition number of the importance sampling distribution.

Definition 4.16

(Definition 3.1 of
[
CP19a
]
)
.

For any unknown distribution
D^{\prime}
over the domain
G
and any function
f\in{\cal F}
, let
f^{(D^{\prime})}(t):=\sqrt{\frac{D(t)}{D^{\prime}(t)}}\cdot f(t)
be the importance sampling function for some known distribution
D
such that

\underset{t\sim D^{\prime}}{\E}\left[|f^{(D^{\prime})}(t)|^{2}\right]=\underset{t\sim D^{\prime}}{\E}\left[\frac{D(t)}{D^{\prime}(t)}|f(t)|^{2}\right]=\underset{t\sim D}{\E}\left[|f(t)|^{2}\right].

Then, we can use samples from
D^{\prime}
to estimate
\|f^{(D^{\prime})}\|_{D^{\prime}}
, which gives an estimate of
\|f\|_{D}
.

When the family
\mathcal{F}
and
D
is clear, we use
K_{\mathsf{IS},D^{\prime}}
to denote the condition number of importance sampling from
D^{\prime}
:

\displaystyle K_{\mathsf{IS},D^{\prime}}=\underset{t}{\sup}\left\{\underset{f\in\mathcal{F}}{\sup}\left\{\frac{|f^{(D^{\prime})}(t)|^{2}}{\|f^{D^{\prime}}\|_{D^{\prime}}^{2}}\right\}\right\}=\underset{t}{\sup}\bigg\{\frac{D(t)}{D^{\prime}(t)}\cdot\underset{f\in\mathcal{F}}{\sup}\big\{\frac{|f(t)|^{2}}{\|f\|_{D}^{2}}\big\}\bigg\}.

(7)

From Definition
4.16
, we know that the efficiency of importance sampling depends on how many samples we need to estimate
\|f^{D^{\prime}}\|_{D^{\prime}}
. The following lemma provide a criteria for judging whether a set of samples gives a good estimation for the norm of function.

Lemma 4.17

(Lemma 4.2 in
[
CP19a
]
)
.

For any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, let
S=\{t_{1},\dotsc,t_{s}\}
and the weight vector
w\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{s}
. Define a matrix
A\in\mathbb{R}^{s\times d}
be the
s\times d
matrix defined as
A_{i,j}=\sqrt{w_{i}}\cdot v_{j}(t_{i})
, where
\{v_{1},\dots,v_{d}\}
is an orthonormal basis for
{\cal F}
. Then

\|h\|^{2}_{S,w}:=\sum_{j=1}^{s}w_{j}\cdot|h(x_{j})|^{2}\in[1\pm\varepsilon]\cdot\|h\|_{D}^{2}\text{ \hskip 10.22217ptfor every }h\in{\mathcal{F}}

if and only if the eigenvalues of
A^{*}A
are in
[1-\varepsilon,1+\varepsilon]
.

The following lemma shows that the sample complexity depends on the condition number
K_{\mathsf{IS},D^{\prime}}
:

Lemma 4.18

(Lemma 6.6 in
[
CP19a
]
)
.

Let
D^{\prime}
be an arbitrary distribution over
G
and let
K_{\mathsf{IS},D^{\prime}}
be the condition number of importance sampling from
D^{\prime}
(defined by Eq. (
7
)).
There exists an absolute constant
C
such that for any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and
\delta\in(0,1)
, let
S=\{t_{1},\dots,t_{s}\}
be a set of i.i.d. samples from the distribution
D^{\prime}
and let
w
be the weight vector defined by
w_{j}=\frac{D(t_{j})}{s\cdot D^{\prime}(t_{j})}
for each
j\in[s]
. Then, as long as

\displaystyle s\geq\frac{C}{\varepsilon^{2}}\cdot K_{\mathsf{IS},D^{\prime}}\log\frac{d}{\delta},

the
s\times d
matrix
A_{i,j}=\sqrt{w_{i}}\cdot v_{j}(t_{i})

satisfies

\|A^{*}A-I\|_{2}\leq\varepsilon\text{ with probability at least }1-\delta.

5
Energy Bounds for Fourier Signals

The energy bound shows that the maximum value of a Fourier sparse signal in a certain interval can be bounded by its energy on the interval. One interesting fact is that the approximation ratio in the energy bound is only relate to the sparsity
k
, and have no relationship with time duration
T
and band-limit
F
. An application of energy bound is preserving the norm, that is what is the least size of set
S
, such that
\|f\|_{S}=\|f\|_{T}
, for any function
f
in a certain function family. The relationship between energy bound and norm preserving can be build by Chernoff bound.

[
BE06
,
Kós08
,
CKPS16
,
CP19b
]
proved energy bounds for sparse Fourier signal under one-dimensional continuous Fourier transform.
We further generalize these results to discrete Fourier sparse signal under discrete Fourier transform and high-dimensional Fourier sparse signal under continuous Fourier transform.

This section is organized as follows:

•

Section
5.1
reviews previous results for one-dimensional continuous Fourier-sparse signals.

•

Section
5.2
proves a new energy bound for high dimensional Fourier-sparse signals, and also gives a nearly matching lower bound.

•

Section
5.3
proves energy bound for discrete Fourier-sparse signals.

•

Section
5.4
builds the connection between energy bound and the concentration property.

5.1
Energy bound for one-dimensional signals

In this section, we review the energy bound proved in prior work
[
BE06
,
Kós08
,
CKPS16
,
CP19b
]
.

[
Kós08
]
proved the following energy bound:

Theorem 5.1

(
[
Kós08
,
CKPS16
]
)
.

Define a family of
F
-band-limit,
k
-sparse Fourier signals:

\displaystyle{\cal F}:=\Big\{x(t)=\sum_{j=1}^{k}v_{j}\cdot e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}t}~\Big|~f_{j}\in\mathbb{R}\cap[-F,F]\Big\}

Then, for any
t\in(-1,1)
,

\displaystyle\underset{x\in{\cal F}}{\sup}\frac{|x(t)|^{2}}{\|x\|_{D}^{2}}\lesssim k^{2}.

[
BE06
]
also proved a time-dependent energy bound for one-dimensional signal:

Theorem 5.2

(
[
BE06
,
CP19a
]
)
.

Define a family of
F
-band-limit,
k
-sparse Fourier signals:

\displaystyle{\cal F}:=\Big\{x(t)=\sum_{j=1}^{k}v_{j}\cdot e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}t}~\Big|~f_{j}\in\mathbb{R}\cap[-F,F]\Big\}

Then, for any
t\in(-1,1)
,

\displaystyle\underset{x\in{\cal F}}{\sup}\frac{|x(t)|^{2}}{\|x\|_{D}^{2}}\lesssim\frac{k}{1-|t|}.

5.2
Energy bound for high-dimensional signals

The goal of this section is to prove Theorem
5.3
, which gives an energy bound for
d
-dimensional Fourier signal. It can be viewed as a
d
-dimensional version of
[
CKPS16
, Lemma 5.1]
. We also prove a lower bound in Lemma
5.5
.

Theorem 5.3

(Energy bound in
d
-dimensional)
.

For any
d
-dimensional
k
-Fourier-sparse signal
x(t):\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
and any duration
T
, we have

\displaystyle\max_{t\in[0,T]^{d}}|x(t)|^{2}\leq k^{O(d)}\|x\|_{T}^{2},

where
\|x\|_{T}^{2}=\frac{1}{T^{d}}\int_{[0,T]^{d}}|x(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we fix
T=1
. Then
\|x\|_{T}^{2}=\int_{[0,1]^{d}}|x(t)|\mathrm{d}t
. Because
\|x\|_{T}^{2}
is the average over the interval
[0,T]^{d}
, if the maximizer
t^{*}=\arg\max_{t\in[0,T]^{d}}|x(t)|^{2}
is not
0^{d}
or
T=1
, we can scale the two intervals
[0^{d},t^{*}]
and
[t^{*},T^{d}]
to
[0,1]
and prove the desired property separately. Hence we assume that
|x(0)|^{2}=\max_{t\in[0,T]}|x(t)|^{2}
in the proof.

In the next a few paragraphs, we show how to use Lemma
5.4
to prove Theorem
5.3
.

We use
0^{d}
to denote a length-
d
vector with
0
everywhere. Due to Lemma
5.4
, we can choose
t_{0}=0^{d}
such that
\forall\tau\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{d}
there exist
C_{1},\cdots,C_{m}\in\mathbb{C}
, and

\displaystyle x(0^{d})=\sum_{j\in[m]}C_{j}\cdot x(j\cdot\tau).

By the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, it implies that for any
\tau
,

\displaystyle|x(0^{d})|^{2}\leq m\sum_{j\in[m]}|C_{j}|^{2}|x(j\cdot\tau)|^{2}

Then, we obtain

\displaystyle|x(0^{d})|^{2}=

\displaystyle~m^{d}\int_{[0,1/m]^{d}}|x(0^{d})|^{2}\mathrm{d}\tau

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle~m^{d}\cdot\int_{[0,1/m]^{d}}\left(m\sum_{j=1}^{m}|x(j\cdot\tau)|^{2}\right)\mathrm{d}\tau

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~m^{d+1}\cdot\sum_{j=1}^{m}\int_{[0,1/m]^{d}}|x(j\cdot\tau)|^{2}\mathrm{d}\tau

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~m^{d+1}\cdot\sum_{j=1}^{m}\frac{1}{j^{d}}\int_{[0,j/m]^{d}}|x(\tau)|^{2}\mathrm{d}\tau

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~m^{d+1}\cdot\sum_{j=1}^{m}\frac{1}{j^{d}}\int_{[0,1]^{d}}|x(\tau)|^{2}\mathrm{d}\tau

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle~m^{d+1}\log m\cdot\|x\|_{T}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~k^{O(d)}\|x\|_{T}^{2},

(8)

where the third step follows by moving
m
outside of the integral and swapping the integration and the summation, the fourth step follows by replacing
j\tau
by
\tau
, the fifth step follows by
j/m\leq 1
, the sixth step follows by
\sum_{j=1}^{m}1/j^{d}\leq\sum_{j=1}^{m}1/j=O(\log m)
and the definition of
\|x\|_{T}^{2}
, and the last step follows from Lemma
5.4
that
m=\mathrm{poly}(k)
.

Thus, we have the desired bound.
∎

The following lemma shows that each point of the signal can be expressed as a linear combination of about
k^{2}
equally spaced signal points.

Lemma 5.4

(
d
-dimensional signal interpolation)
.

For any
k
and
d
, there exists
m=O(k^{2}\log k)
such that for any
d
-dimensional
k
-Fourier-sparse signal
x(t)
, any
t_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}_{\geq 0}
and
\tau\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{d}
, there always exist
C_{1},C_{2},\cdots,C_{m}\in\mathbb{C}
such that the following properties hold,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Property~I}

\displaystyle\quad~|C_{j}|\leq 11~\text{~for~all~}j\in[m],

\displaystyle\mathrm{Property~II}

\displaystyle\quad~x(t_{0})=\sum_{j\in[m]}C_{j}\cdot x(t_{0}+j\cdot\tau).

Proof.

Consider a specific signal
x(t):=\sum_{i=1}^{k}v_{i}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{i}^{\top}t}
for
t\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, where
f_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
are given. We fix
t_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\tau\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and then rewrite
x(t_{0}+j\cdot\tau)
as a polynomial of
b_{i}:=v_{i}\cdot e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{i}^{\top}t_{0}}
and
z_{i}:=e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{i}^{\top}\tau}
for each
i\in[k]
.

\displaystyle x(t_{0}+j\cdot\tau)=

\displaystyle~\sum_{i=1}^{k}v_{i}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{i}^{\top}(t_{0}+j\tau)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\sum_{i=1}^{k}v_{i}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{i}^{\top}t_{0}}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{i}^{\top}j\tau}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\sum_{i=1}^{k}b_{i}\cdot z_{i}^{j}.

where the last step follows from the definition of
b_{i}
and
z_{i}
.

Given
k
and
z_{1},\cdots,z_{k}
, let
P(z)=\sum_{j=0}^{m}c_{j}z^{j}
be the degree
m
-polynomial in
[
CKPS16
, Lemma 5.4]
.

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{m}c_{j}x(t_{0}+j\tau)=

\displaystyle~\sum_{j=0}^{m}c_{j}\sum_{i=1}^{k}b_{i}\cdot z_{i}^{j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\sum_{i=1}^{k}b_{i}\sum_{j=0}^{m}c_{j}\cdot z_{i}^{j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\sum_{i=1}^{k}b_{i}P(z_{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~0,

where the last step follows by Property I of
P(z)
in
[
CKPS16
, Lemma 5.4]
.

By Property II and III in
[
CKPS16
, Lemma 5.4]
, we have
x(t_{0})=-\sum_{j=1}^{m}c_{j}x(t_{0}+j\tau)
.
∎

The energy bound in Theorem
5.3
for
d
-dimensional signals is nearly optimal due to a
k^{\Omega(d)}
lower bound as follows.
12
12

12

The proof is due to Yang P. Liu.

Lemma 5.5
.

Given
d\geq 1,\delta\in(0,0.1),k\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
k\geq O(d^{1+\delta})
. Then, there is a
d
-dimensional
k
-Fourier-sparse signal
x(t):\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
and a duration
T
such that,

\displaystyle\max_{t\in[0,T]^{d}}|x(t)|^{2}\geq k^{\Omega(\delta d)}\|x(t)\|_{T}^{2}.

Proof.

We consider the following construction of
x(t)
:

\displaystyle x(t):=2^{-k}(1+e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\langle f_{0},t\rangle})^{k},

where
f_{0}={\bf 1}/(100dT)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

It is easy to see that
x
is a
k
-Fourier sparse signal, and

\displaystyle|x(t)|^{2}=

\displaystyle~2^{-2k}|1+e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\langle f_{0},t\rangle}|^{2k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~2^{-2k}\big((1+e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\langle f_{0},t\rangle})(1+e^{-2\pi\mathbf{i}\langle f_{0},t\rangle})\big)^{k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~2^{-2k}\big(2+e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\langle f_{0},t\rangle}+e^{-2\pi\mathbf{i}\langle f_{0},t\rangle}\big)^{k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~2^{-2k}\cdot 2^{k}(1+\cos(2\pi\langle f_{0},t\rangle))^{k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\Big(\frac{1+\cos(2\pi\langle f_{0},t\rangle)}{2}\Big)^{k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\cos(\pi\langle f_{0},t\rangle)^{2k},

where the first step follows from the definition, the second step follows from
|z|^{2}=z\overline{z}
, the third step is straightforward, the fourth step follows from
e^{ia}+e^{-ia}=2\cos(a)
, the fifth step is straightforward, the last step follows from
(\cos(a)+1)/2=\cos(a/2)^{2}
.

Then, we know that

\displaystyle\max_{t\in[0,T]^{d}}|x(t)|^{2}=|x({\bf 0})|^{2}=1.

(9)

It remains to upper bound

\displaystyle\|x(t)\|_{T}^{2}=T^{-d}\int_{[0,T]^{d}}|x(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t=T^{-d}\int_{[0,T]^{d}}\cos(\pi\langle f_{0},t\rangle)^{2k}\mathrm{d}t.

Let
r
be a parameter. We have

\displaystyle\int_{[0,T]^{d}}\cos(\pi\langle f_{0},t\rangle)^{2k}\mathrm{d}t=

\displaystyle~\int_{W_{d}(r)}\cos(\pi\langle f_{0},t\rangle)^{2k}\mathrm{d}t+\int_{[0,T]\backslash W_{d}(r)}\cos(\pi\langle f_{0},t\rangle)^{2k}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\int_{W_{d}(r)}1\cdot\mathrm{d}t+T^{d}\cdot\max_{t\in[0,T]\backslash W_{d}(r)}~\cos(\pi\langle f_{0},t\rangle)^{2k},

where
W_{d}(r):=\{t\in[0,T]^{d}~|~t_{1}+\cdots+t_{d}\leq r\}
.

We first bound the second term:

\displaystyle\cos(\pi\langle f_{0},t\rangle)^{2k}\leq\exp\big(-\pi^{2}k\langle f_{0},t\rangle^{2}\big)\leq\exp(-\Omega(kr^{2}/(dT)^{2})),

where the first step follows from Fact
4.5
, and the second step follows from the definition of
f_{0}
and
t\notin W_{d}(r)
. Hence,

\displaystyle T^{d}\cdot\max_{t\in[0,T]\backslash W_{d}(r)}~\cos(\pi\langle f_{0},t\rangle)^{2k}\leq T^{d}\cdot\exp(-\Omega(kr^{2}/(dT)^{2})).

(10)

Next, we bound the first term, which is equal to the volume of
W_{d}(r)
. Note that
W_{d}(r)
is contained in the following simplex:

\displaystyle P_{d}(r):=\{t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}~|~t_{1}+\cdots+t_{d}\leq r\}.

Thus, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Vol}(W_{d}(r))\leq

\displaystyle~\mathrm{Vol}(P_{d}(r))\leq r^{d}\cdot\mathrm{Vol}(P_{d}(1))

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~r^{d}/d!

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~O(r/d)^{d},

(11)

where the first step is straightforward, the second step follows from the scaling of the volume, the third step follows from a well-known fact on the volume of a
d
-dimensional simplex
P_{d}(1)=1/d!
(see e.g.
[
Ste66
]
), and the last step follows from Stirling’s approximation.

Combining Eqs. (
10
) and (
5.2
) together, we get that

\displaystyle T^{-d}\|x(t)\|_{T}^{2}\leq O(\frac{r}{dT})^{d}+\exp(-\Omega(kr^{2}/(dT)^{2})).

By taking
r=Tdk^{-\delta/6}
, we have

\displaystyle O(\frac{r}{dT})^{d}=O(k^{-\delta/6})^{d}=k^{-\Omega(\delta d)},

and

\displaystyle\exp(-\Omega(kr^{2}/(dT)^{2}))=

\displaystyle~\exp(-\Omega(k^{1-\delta/3}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\exp(-\Omega(d^{(1+\delta)(1-\delta/3)}))

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\exp(-\Omega(\delta d\log k))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~k^{-\Omega(d)},

where the first step is straightforward, the second step follows from
k=O(d^{1+\delta})
, and the last step follows from
\delta\in(0,1)
.

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle T^{-d}\|x(t)\|_{T}^{2}\leq k^{-\Omega(d)}=k^{-\Omega(d)}\cdot\max_{t\in[0,T]^{d}}~|x(t)|^{2},

where the last step follows from Eq. (
9
).

The lemma is then proved.

∎

5.3
Energy bound for discrete Fourier signals

Recall the definition of one-dimensional discrete sparse Fourier signal: for
t\in\{0,1,\dots,n-1\}
,

\displaystyle x_{t}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\widehat{x}_{f_{i}}\exp\left(\frac{2\pi\mathbf{i}}{n}f_{i}t\right),~f_{i}\in[n]

(12)

More generally, for
d\geq 1
, the
d
-dimensional discrete sparse Fourier signal can be defined as follows. Let
n=p^{d}
where both
p
and
d
are positive integers. Recall the definition of high-dimensional discrete sparse Fourier signal (see e.g.
[
NSW19
]
):

\displaystyle x_{t}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\widehat{x}_{f_{i}}\exp\left(\frac{2\pi\mathbf{i}}{p}\langle f_{i},t\rangle\right)~~~\forall t\in[p]^{d},

(13)

where each
f_{i}\in[p]^{d}
.

In this section, we prove the following discrete Fourier signals energy bound that works for any dimension:

Theorem 5.6

(Discrete
d
-dimensional Fourier energy bound)
.

For
d\geq 1
and any discrete
d
-dimensional
k
-sparse Fourier signal
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
, we have

\displaystyle\|x\|_{\infty}^{2}\leq k\cdot\frac{\|x\|_{2}^{2}}{n}

Proof.

\displaystyle\|x\|_{\infty}^{2}\leq\frac{1}{n^{2}}\|\widehat{x}\|_{1}^{2}\leq\frac{k}{n^{2}}\|\widehat{x}\|_{2}^{2}=\frac{k}{n}\|x\|_{2}^{2},

where the first step follows from Claim
5.7
, the second step follows from Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, and the last step follows from Theorem
5.8
.
∎

Claim 5.7
.

For any
d\geq 1
and any discrete
d
-dimensional Fourier signal
x
,

\displaystyle\|x\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{1}{n}\|\widehat{x}\|_{1}.

Proof.

By triangle inequality,

\displaystyle|x(t)|\leq\frac{1}{n}\left|\sum_{i=1}^{k}\widehat{x}_{i}\exp\left(\frac{2\pi\mathbf{i}}{p}\langle f_{i},t\rangle\right)\right|\leq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{k}|\widehat{x}_{i}|=\frac{1}{n}\|\widehat{x}\|_{1}.

∎

Theorem 5.8

(Parseval’s theorem)
.

For any
d\geq 1
and any discrete
d
-dimensional Fourier signal (Eq. (
13
)),

\displaystyle\|x\|_{2}^{2}=\frac{1}{n}\|\widehat{x}\|_{2}^{2}.

5.4
Energy bounds imply concentrations

By using Chernoff bound, we prove the following lemma to show the performance of uniformly sampling.

5.4.1
Continuous case

Lemma 5.9
.

Let
d\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
. Let
R
be a parameter. Given any function
x(t):\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
with
\underset{t\in[0,T]^{d}}{\max}|x(t)|^{2}\leq R\|x(t)\|_{T}^{2}
. Let
S
denote a set of points chosen uniformly at random from
[0,T]^{d}
. We have that

\displaystyle\mathsf{Pr}\left[\left|\frac{1}{|S|}\sum_{i\in S}|x(t_{i})|^{2}-\|x(t)\|_{T}^{2}]\right|\geq\varepsilon\|x(t)\|_{T}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\exp(-\Omega(\varepsilon^{2}|S|/R)),

where
\|x(t)\|_{T}^{2}=\frac{1}{T^{d}}\int_{[0,T]^{d}}|x(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t
.

Proof.

Let
M
denote
\underset{t\in[0,T]^{d}}{\max}|x(t)|^{2}
. Replacing
X_{i}
by
\frac{|x(t_{i})|^{2}}{M}
and
n
by
|S|
in Lemma
4.1
, we obtain that

\displaystyle\mathsf{Pr}[|X-\mu|>\varepsilon\mu]\leq 2\exp(-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{3}\mu)

The above equation implies

\displaystyle\mathsf{Pr}\left[\left|\sum_{i\in S}\frac{|x(t_{i})|^{2}}{M}-|S|\frac{\|x(t)\|_{T}^{2}}{M}\right|>\varepsilon|S|\frac{\|x(t)\|_{T}^{2}}{M}\right]\leq 2\exp(-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{3}\mu)

Multiplying
M
on the both sides

\displaystyle\mathsf{Pr}\left[\left|\frac{1}{|S|}\sum_{i\in S}|x(t_{i})|^{2}-\|x(t)\|_{T}^{2}\right|\geq\varepsilon\|x(t)\|_{T}^{2}\right]\leq 2\exp(-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{3}\mu)

Applying bound on
\mu

\displaystyle\mathsf{Pr}\left[\left|\frac{1}{|S|}\sum_{i\in S}|x(t_{i})|^{2}-\|x(t)\|_{T}^{2}\right|\geq\varepsilon\|x(t)\|_{T}^{2}\right]\leq 2\exp(-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{3}|S|\frac{\|x(t)\|_{T}^{2}}{M})

which is less than
2\exp(-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{3}|S|/R)
, thus completes the proof.
∎

5.4.2
Discrete case

Lemma 5.10
.

Let
R
be a parameter. Given any function
x\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
with
\|x\|_{\infty}^{2}\leq R\|x\|_{2}^{2}/n
. Let
S
denote a set of points chosen uniformly at random from
[n]
. We have that

\displaystyle\mathsf{Pr}\left[\left|\frac{1}{|S|}\sum_{t\in S}|x_{t}|^{2}-n^{-1}\|x\|_{2}^{2}]\right|\geq\varepsilon n^{-1}\|x\|_{2}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\exp(-\Omega(\varepsilon^{2}|S|/R)).

Proof.

Let
M
denote
\underset{t\in[n]}{\max}|x_{t}|^{2}
. Replacing
X_{i}
by
\frac{|x_{t}|^{2}}{M}
and
n
by
|S|
in Lemma
4.1
, we obtain that

\displaystyle\mathsf{Pr}[|X-\mu|>\varepsilon\mu]\leq 2\exp(-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{3}\mu)

The above equation implies that

\displaystyle\mathsf{Pr}\left[\left|\sum_{t\in S}\frac{|x_{t}|^{2}}{M}-|S|\frac{\|x\|_{2}^{2}}{nM}\right|>\varepsilon|S|\frac{\|x\|_{2}^{2}}{nM}\right]\leq 2\exp(-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{3}\mu)

Multiplying the normalization factor on both sides,

\displaystyle\mathsf{Pr}\left[\left|\frac{1}{|S|}\sum_{t\in S}|x_{t}|^{2}-n^{-1}\|x\|_{2}^{2}\right|\geq\varepsilon n^{-1}\|x\|_{2}^{2}\right]\leq 2\exp(-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{3}\mu)

Applying bound on
\mu

\displaystyle\mathsf{Pr}\left[\left|\frac{1}{|S|}\sum_{t\in S}|x_{t}|^{2}-n^{-1}\|x\|_{2}^{2}\right|\geq\varepsilon n^{-1}\|x\|_{2}^{2}\right]\leq 2\exp(-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{3}|S|\frac{\|x\|_{2}^{2}}{nM})

which is less than
2\exp(-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{3}|S|/R)
, thus completes the proof.
∎

6
Oblivious Sketching Fourier Sparse Signals

In this section, we show an intermediate step in the reduction from Frequency estimation to Signal estimation: constructing a small sketching subset
S
of the time domain
obliviously
(without making any query to the signal), so that any signal in the family
{\cal F}
discretized by
S
has norm close to the original continuous signal.
More formally, we define the
oblivious sketching Fourier signal problem
as follows:

Problem 6.1

(Oblivious sketching Fourier sparse signal problem)
.

Let
{\cal F}
be a family of Fourier-sparse signals.
Let
\varepsilon\in(0,0.1)
denote the accuracy parameter. The goal is to find a set
S=\{t_{1},\dots,t_{s}\}
in the time domain of size
s
such that, with high probability, for any signal
x\in{\cal F}
, it holds that

\displaystyle(1-\varepsilon)\|x\|_{T}\leq\|x\|_{S}\leq(1+\varepsilon)\|x\|_{T},

where

\displaystyle\|x\|_{T}^{2}:=\frac{1}{T^{d}}\int_{[0,T]^{d}}|x(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t,~~\mathrm{and}~~\|x\|^{2}_{S}:=\frac{1}{|S|}\sum_{i\in[s]}|x(t_{i})|^{2}.

We remark that the concentration inequalities in Section
5.4
do not give a small sketching directly. To apply concentration inequalities for all signals in
{\cal F}
, we need an
\varepsilon
-net, which will increase the sketching size by a factor of
\mathrm{poly}(k)
.

In Section
6.1
, we show a unified approach to obtain oblivious sketching using uniform samples for general signals. Then, in Section
6.2
, we show how to sketch one-dimensional signals with nearly-optimal size using importance sampling.

6.1
A unified approach via uniform sampling

In this sub-section, we prove a general result of oblivious sketching for discrete and continuous Fourier-sparse signals. The technical tools we employ are Fourier energy bounds and importance sampling in Section
4.4
.

Lemma 6.2

(Oblivious sketching via uniform sampling)
.

Let
{\cal F}\subset\{x(t):{\cal G}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}\}
be a family of signals of dimension
k
. Suppose the energy bound
R\geq\sup_{t\in{\cal G}}\sup_{x\in{\cal F}}\frac{|x(t)|}{\|x(t)\|_{T}}
holds. For any
\varepsilon,\rho\in(0,1)
, let
S=\{t_{1},t_{2},\dots,t_{s}\}
be a set of i.i.d. samples chosen uniformly at random over the time domain
{\cal G}
of size

\displaystyle s\geq\Omega\left(\varepsilon^{-2}R\log(k/\rho)\right).

Then, with probability at least
1-\rho
, it holds that for all
x\in{\cal F}
,

\displaystyle(1-\varepsilon)\|x\|_{T}\leq\|x\|_{S}\leq(1+\varepsilon)\|x\|_{T}.

Proof.

To show that sampling from the distribution
D=\mathrm{Uniform}({\cal G})
gives a good sketch, we apply Lemma
4.18
with
D^{\prime}=D
,
d=k
,
\delta=\rho
,
w_{i}=1/s
, which implies that the matrix
A\in\mathbb{C}^{s\times k}
defined by
A_{i,j}:=\sqrt{w_{i}}\cdot v_{j}(t_{i})
satisfies:

\displaystyle\|A^{*}A-I\|_{2}\leq\varepsilon

(14)

with probability at least
1-\rho
, as long as
s=\Omega(\varepsilon^{-2}K_{\mathsf{IS},D}\log(k/{\rho}))
.

Under the condition that Eq. (
14
) holds, Lemma
4.17
shows that

\displaystyle(1-\varepsilon)\|x\|_{T}^{2}\leq\|{x}\|^{2}_{S}\leq(1+\varepsilon)\|x\|_{T}^{2}~~~\forall x\in{\cal F},

which proves the oblivious sketching guarantee.

It remains to upper-bound the condition number
K_{\mathsf{IS},D}
. By Definition
4.16
, we have

\displaystyle K_{\mathsf{IS},D}=

\displaystyle~\underset{t\in{\cal G}}{\sup}~\underset{x\in\mathcal{F}}{\sup}~\frac{|x(t)|^{2}}{\|x\|_{T}^{2}}\leq R.

Thus, we get that

\displaystyle s=\Omega\left(\varepsilon^{-2}R\log(k/\rho)\right).

The lemma is then proved.
∎

Using the
d
-dimensional Fourier-sparse signals’ energy bound (Theorem
5.3
) and the discrete Fourier-sparse signals’ energy bound (Theorem
5.6
), we immediately obtain the following corollaries:

Corollary 6.3

(Oblivious sketching high-dimensional continuous signal)
.

Let
d>1
be the dimension of the signal. Let
f_{1},\dots,f_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. For any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, let
S_{d}
be a set of i.i.d. samples chosen uniformly at random over
[0,T]^{d}
of size
|S_{d}|\geq\varepsilon^{-2}k^{O(d)}\log(k/\rho)
. Let
V:=\{\exp(2\pi\mathbf{i}\langle f_{i},t\rangle)~|~i\in[k]\}
. Then, with probability at least
1-\rho
, for all
x\in\mathrm{span}\{V\}
, we have

(1-\varepsilon)\|x\|_{T}\leq\|{x}\|_{S_{d}}\leq(1+\varepsilon)\|{x}\|_{T}.

Corollary 6.4

(Oblivious sketching discrete signal)
.

For
d\geq 1
, let
n=p^{d}
for some positive integer
p
. Let
k\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
and
f_{1},\dots,f_{k}\in[p]^{d}
. Define
V:=\left\{(e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\langle f_{i},t\rangle/p})_{t\in[p]^{d}}~{|}~\forall i\in[k]\right\}\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{[p]^{d}}
.
For any
\varepsilon,\rho\in(0,1)
, let
S
be a set of i.i.d. samples chosen uniformly at random over
[n]
of size
|S|\geq O(\varepsilon^{-2}k\log(k/\rho))
. Then, with probability at least
1-\rho
, for all
u\in\mathrm{span}\{V\}
, we have

(1-\varepsilon)\|u\|^{2}_{2}\leq n\|{u}\|^{2}_{S}\leq(1+\varepsilon)\|{u}\|^{2}_{2},

where
\|u\|_{S}^{2}=\sum_{i\in S}|u_{i}|^{2}/|S|
.

6.2
Weighted oblivious sketching one-dimensional signals

For one-dimensional signals, using uniform sampling (Lemma
6.2
) and the energy bound (Theorem
5.1
) gives a sketching set of size at least
\varepsilon^{-2}k^{2}
. Motivated by
[
CP19a
]
, we show a more efficient sketching method using only
\varepsilon^{-2}k
samples by assigning different weights to each sample point.
In the following lemma, we give a sketch for any one-dimensional Fourier sparse signal with nearly-optimal size:

Lemma 6.5

(Nearly-optimal weighted sketch for one-dimensional signals)
.

For
k\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
, define a probability distribution
{\cal D}(t)
as follows:

\displaystyle{\cal D}(t):=\begin{cases}{c}/(1-|t/T|),&\text{ for }|t|\leq T(1-{1}/k)\\
c\cdot k,&\text{ for }|t|\in[T(1-{1}/k),T]\end{cases}

(15)

where
c=\Theta(T^{-1}\log^{-1}(k))
is a normalization factor such that
\int_{-T}^{T}{\cal D}(t)\mathrm{d}t=1
.

For any
f_{1},\cdots,f_{k}\in[-F,F]
, let
{\cal F}:=\Big\{x(t)=\sum_{j=1}^{k}v_{j}\cdot e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}t}~\Big|~v_{j}\in\mathbb{C}\Big\}
. For any
\varepsilon,\rho\in(0,1)
, let
S_{{\cal D}}=\{t_{1},\cdots,t_{s}\}
be a set of i.i.d. samples from
{\cal D}(t)
of size
s\geq O(\varepsilon^{-2}k\log(k)\log(k/\rho))
. Let the weight vector
w\in\mathbb{R}^{s}
be defined by
w_{i}:=2/(Ts{\cal D}(t_{i}))
for
i\in[s]
. Then with probability at least
1-\rho
, we have

(1-\varepsilon)\|x\|_{T}\leq\|x\|_{S_{\cal D},w}\leq(1+\varepsilon)\|x\|_{T},

where
\|x\|_{T}^{2}:=\frac{1}{2T}\int_{-T}^{T}|x(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t
.
13
13

13

We use time duration
[-T,T]
for convenience. It is easy to transform to
[0,T]
by shifting and re-scaling.

Proof.

Let
\{v_{1}(t),v_{2}(t),\cdots,v_{k}(t)\}
be an orthonormal basis for
{\cal F}
with respect to the distribution
{\cal D}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}{\cal D}(t)\cdot v_{i}(t)\overline{v_{j}(t)}\mathrm{d}t=~{\bf 1}_{i=j},\quad\forall i,j\in[k].

We first prove that the distribution
{\cal D}
is well-defined. By the condition that
\int_{-T}^{T}{\cal D}(t)\mathrm{d}t=1
, we have

\displaystyle 2\int_{0}^{T(1-{1}/({k}))}\frac{c}{(1-|t/T|)}\mathrm{d}t+2\int_{T(1-{1}/({k}))}^{T}c\cdot k^{2}k\mathrm{d}t=1,

which implies that

\displaystyle c^{-1}=

\displaystyle~2\int_{0}^{T(1-{1}/k)}\frac{1}{(1-|t/T|)}\mathrm{d}t+2\int_{T(1-{1}/k)}^{T}k^{2}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~2T{\log k}+2T

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\Theta(T\log(k)).

Thus, we get that
c=\Theta(T^{-1}\log^{-1}(k))
.

To show that sampling from distribution
{\cal D}
gives a good weighted sketch, we will use some technical tools in Section
4.4
.
Applying Lemma
4.18
with
D^{\prime}={\cal D}
,
D=\mathrm{Uniform}([-T,T])
,
d=k
,
\delta=\rho
, we have that, with probability at least
1-\rho
, the matrix
A\in\mathbb{C}^{s\times k}
defined by
A_{i,j}:=\sqrt{w_{i}}\cdot v_{j}(t_{i})
satisfying

\displaystyle\|A^{*}A-I\|_{2}\leq\varepsilon,

as long as
s\geq\Omega(\varepsilon^{-2}K_{\mathsf{IS},D^{\prime}}\log(k/{\rho}))
, where
w_{i}=\frac{D(t_{i})}{s\cdot D^{\prime}(t_{i})}=\frac{2}{Ts{\cal D}(t_{i})}
. Then, by Lemma
4.17
, it implies that for every
x\in{\mathcal{F}}
,

\displaystyle(1-\varepsilon)\|x\|_{T}^{2}\leq\|{x}\|^{2}_{S_{\cal D},w}\leq(1+\varepsilon)\|x\|_{T}^{2}.

It remains to upper-bound the condition number
K_{\mathsf{IS},D^{\prime}}
(see Definition
4.16
):

\displaystyle K_{\mathsf{IS},D^{\prime}}:=

\displaystyle~\underset{t}{\sup}\{\frac{D(t)}{D^{\prime}(t)}\cdot\underset{f\in\mathcal{F}}{\sup}\{\frac{|f(t)|^{2}}{\|f\|_{D}^{2}}\}\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\underset{t}{\sup}\{\frac{1}{2T{\cal D}(t)}\cdot\underset{f\in\mathcal{F}}{\sup}\{\frac{|f(t)|^{2}}{\|f\|_{T}^{2}}\}\}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\underset{t}{\sup}\{\frac{1}{2T{\cal D}(t)}\cdot{\min}\{\frac{k}{1-|t/T|},k^{2}\}\}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~{\max}\{\frac{(1-|t/T|)}{2cT}\frac{k}{1-|t/T|},\frac{1}{2cTk}k^{2}\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\frac{k}{2cT}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~O(k\log k),

where the first step follows from the definition, the second step follows from
D(t)=\mathrm{Uniform}([-T,T])(t)=\frac{1}{2T}
, the third step follows from Theorem
5.1
and Theorem
5.2
, and the remaining steps follow from direct calculations. Thus, we get that

\displaystyle s\geq\Omega\left(\varepsilon^{-2}k\log(k)\log(k/\rho)\right).

The lemma is then proved.
∎

7
Fast Implementation of Well-Balanced Sampling Procedure

Well-balanced sampling procedure was first defined in
[
CP19a
]
to study the active linear regression problem. Our signal estimation algorithm will call it as a sub-procedure. In this section, we give a fast implementation of well-balanced sampling procedure based on the Randomized BSS algorithm
[
BSS12
,
LS15
]
.

First, we restate the definition of well-balanced sampling procedure in
[
CP19a
]
.

Definition 7.1

(Well-balanced sampling procedure (WBSP),
[
CP19a
]
)
.

Given a linear family
\mathcal{F}
and underlying distribution
D
, let
P
be a random sampling procedure that terminates in
m
iterations (
m
is not necessarily fixed) and provides a coefficient
\alpha_{i}
and a distribution
D_{i}
to sample
x_{i}\sim D_{i}
in every iteration
i\in[m]
.

We say
P
is an
\varepsilon
-WBSP if it satisfies the following two properties:

1.

With probability
0.9
, for weight
w_{i}=\alpha_{i}\cdot\frac{D(x_{i})}{D_{i}(x_{i})}
of each
i\in[m]
,

\sum_{i=1}^{m}w_{i}\cdot|h(x_{i})|^{2}\in\left[1-10\sqrt{\varepsilon},1+10\sqrt{\varepsilon}\right]\cdot\|h\|_{D}^{2}\quad\forall h\in\mathcal{F}.

2.

The coefficients always have
\sum_{i=1}^{m}\alpha_{i}\leq\frac{5}{4}
and
\alpha_{i}\cdot K_{\mathsf{IS},D_{i}}\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}
for all
i\in[m]
.

This definition describes a general sampling procedure that uses a few samples to represent the whole continuous signal, and the sampling procedure should satisfy two properties: one guarantees that the norm of any function in a function family is preserved, and another guarantees that the norm of noise is also preserved.

In Section
7.1
, we review some results in
[
CP19a
]
and show that WBSP can be implemented via randomized spectral sparsification. In Section
7.2
, we design a data structure and improve the time efficiency of the WBSP. In Section
7.3
, we discover a tradeoff between the preprocessing cost and the query cost, which can improve the space complexity.

7.1
Randomized BSS implies a WBSP

In this section, we review the result of
[
CP19a
]
, which shows that the Randomized BSS algorithm
[
BSS12
,
LS15
]
implies a well-balanced sampling procedure.

Lemma 7.2

(Lemma 5.1 in
[
CP19a
]
)
.

Let
G
be any domain.
Given any dimension
d
linear function family
\mathcal{F}
of function
f:G\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{F}}=\{f(t)=\sum_{j=1}^{d}v_{j}u_{j}(t)|v_{j}\in\mathbb{C}\},

where
u_{j}:G\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
.
Given any distribution
D
over
G
, and any
\varepsilon>0
, there exists an efficient procedure (Algorithm
2
) that runs in
O(\varepsilon^{-1}d^{3}|G|+\varepsilon^{-1}d^{\omega+1})
time and outputs a set
S\subseteq G
and weight
w
such that

•

|S|=O(d/\varepsilon),~w\in\mathbb{R}^{|S|}
,

•

the procedure is an
\varepsilon
-WBSP,

holds with probability
1-\frac{1}{200}
.

Algorithm 2

A well-balanced sampling procedure based on Randomized BSS (see
[
CP19a
]
)

1:

procedure

RandBSS
(
d,\mathcal{F},D,\varepsilon
)

2:

Find an orthonormal basis
v_{1},\ldots,v_{d}
of
\mathcal{F}
under
D

3:

Set
\gamma\leftarrow\sqrt{\varepsilon}/3
and
\textsf{mid}\leftarrow\frac{4d/\gamma}{1/(1-\gamma)-1/(1+\gamma)}

4:

j\leftarrow 0,B_{0}\leftarrow 0

5:

l_{0}\leftarrow-2d/\gamma,u_{0}\leftarrow 2d/\gamma

6:

while

u_{j+1}-l_{j+1}<8d/\gamma

do

7:

\Phi_{j}\leftarrow\mathrm{tr}[(u_{j}I-B_{j})^{-1}]+\mathrm{tr}[(B_{j}-l_{j}I)^{-1}]

\triangleright
The potential function at iteration
j
.

8:

Set the coefficient
\alpha_{j}\leftarrow\frac{\gamma}{\Phi_{j}}\cdot\frac{1}{\textsf{mid}}

9:

Set
v(x)\leftarrow\big(v_{1}(x),\ldots,v_{d}(x)\big)

10:

for

x\in\mathrm{supp}(D)

do

11:

Set the distribution

D_{j}(x)\leftarrow D(x)\cdot\bigg(v(x)^{\top}(u_{j}I-B_{j})^{-1}v(x)+v(x)^{\top}(B_{j}-l_{j}I)^{-1}v(x)\bigg)/\Phi_{j}

12:

end

for

13:

Sample
x_{j}\sim D_{j}
and set a scale
s_{j}\leftarrow\frac{\gamma}{\Phi_{j}}\cdot\frac{D(x_{j})}{D_{j}(x_{j})}

14:

B_{j+1}\leftarrow B_{j}+s_{j}\cdot v(x_{j})v(x_{j})^{\top}

15:

u_{j+1}\leftarrow u_{j}+\frac{\gamma}{\Phi_{j}(1-\gamma)},\quad l_{j+1}\leftarrow l_{j}+\frac{\gamma}{\Phi_{j}(1+\gamma)}

16:

j\leftarrow j+1

17:

end

while

18:

m\leftarrow j

19:

Assign the weight
w_{j}\leftarrow s_{j}/\textsf{mid}
for each
x_{j}

20:

return

\{x_{1},x_{2},\cdots,x_{m}\},w

21:

end

procedure

7.2
Fast implementation of WBSP

In this section, we give a fast implementation of Algorithm
2
:

Theorem 7.3

(Fast implementation of WBSP)
.

Let
G
be any domain.
Given any dimension
d
linear function family
\mathcal{F}
of function
f:G\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{F}}=\{f(t)=\sum_{j=1}^{d}v_{j}u_{j}(t)|v_{j}\in\mathbb{C}\},

where
u_{j}:G\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
.
Given any distribution
D
over
G
, and any
\varepsilon>0
, there exists an efficient procedure (Algorithm
3
) that runs in
O(d^{2}|G|+\varepsilon^{-1}d^{3}\log|G|+\varepsilon^{-1}d^{\omega+1})
time and outputs a set
S\subseteq G
and weight
w\in\mathbb{R}^{|S|}
such that the following properties hold with probability at least 0.995:

•

|S|=O(d/\varepsilon)
,

•

the procedure is an
\varepsilon
-WBSP.

Algorithm 3

Our fast implementation of well-balanced sampling procedure

procedure

(

)
\triangleright

7.3

v_{1},\ldots,v_{d}
of
\mathcal{F}
under

and

7:

\delta\leftarrow 1/\mathrm{poly}(d)

\triangleright

v(x)=\big(v_{1}(x),\ldots,v_{d}(x)\big)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\mathrm{DS}.\textsc{Init}(|D|,d,\{v(x_{1}),\cdots,v(x_{|D|})\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d},\{D(x_{1}),\dots,D(x_{|D|})\}\subset\mathbb{R})

\triangleright
Algorithm
4

/*Iterative step*/

11:

while

u_{j+1}-l_{j+1}<8d/\gamma

do

\triangleright

j
.

\triangleright

q\in[|D|]
, Algorithm
4

and set a scale

17:

18:

19:

20:

end

while

21:

m\leftarrow j

22:

Assign the weight
w_{j}\leftarrow s_{j}/\textsf{mid}
for each
x_{j}

23:

return

\{{\sf x}_{1},{\sf x}_{2},\cdots,{\sf x}_{m}\},w

24:

end

procedure

Our algorithm is based on a data structure for solving the
online quadratic-form sampling problem
defined as follows:

Problem 7.4

(Online Quadratic-Form Sampling Problem)
.

Given
n
vectors
v_{1},\dots,v_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
n
coefficients
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}
, for any PSD matrix
A\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
, output a sample
i\in[n]
from the following distribution
{\cal D}_{A}
:

\displaystyle\Pr_{{\cal D}_{A}}[i]:=\frac{\alpha_{i}\cdot v_{i}^{\top}Av_{i}}{\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}\cdot v_{j}^{\top}Av_{j}}~~~\forall i\in[n].

(16)

Theorem 7.5
.

There is a data structure (Algorithm
4
) that uses
O(nd^{2})
spaces for the Online Quadratic-Form Sampling Problem with the following procedures:

•

Init
(n,d,\{v_{1},\dots,v_{n}\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d},\{\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}\}\subset\mathbb{R})
: the data structure preprocesses in time
O(nd^{2})
.

•

Query
(A\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d})
: Given a PSD matrix
A
, the
Query
operation samples
i\in[n]
exactly from the probability distribution
{\cal D}_{A}
defined in Problem
7.4
in
O(d^{2}\log n)
-time.

Proof.

The pseudo-code of the algorithm is given as Algorithm
4
. The idea is to build a binary tree such that each node has an interval in
[l,\dots,r]\subset[1,\dots,n]
and stores a matrix
\sum_{i=l}^{r}\alpha_{i}\cdot v_{i}v_{i}^{\top}
. For each internal node with interval
[l,\dots,r]
, its left child node has interval
[l,\dots,\lfloor(l+r)/2\rfloor]
, and its right child node has interval
[\lfloor(l+r)/2\rfloor+1,\dots,r]
.

We first prove the correctness. Suppose the output of
Query
is
i\in[n]
. We compute its probability. Let
u_{0}=\mathsf{root},u_{1},\dots,u_{t}
be the path from the root of the tree to the leaf with
\mathrm{id}=i
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\Pr[u_{t}]=\prod_{j=1}^{t}\Pr[u_{j}|u_{j-1}]=\prod_{j=1}^{t}\frac{\sum_{k=l_{j}}^{r_{j}}\alpha_{k}\cdot v_{k}^{\top}Av_{k}}{\sum_{k=l_{j-1}}^{r_{j-1}}\alpha_{k}\cdot v_{k}^{\top}Av_{k}}=\frac{\alpha_{i}\cdot v_{i}^{\top}Av_{i}}{\sum_{k=1}^{n}\alpha_{k}\cdot v_{k}^{\top}Av_{k}},

where
[l_{j},\dots,r_{j}]
is the range of the node
u_{j}
, the first step follows from the conditional probability, the second step follows from Line
34
in Algorithm
4
, and the last step follows from the telescoping products. Hence, we get that

\displaystyle\Pr[\textsc{Query}(A)=i]=\Pr_{{\cal D}_{A}}[i]~~~\forall i\in[n].

Hence, the sampling distribution is the same as the Online Quadratic-Form Sampling Problem’s distribution.

For the running time, in the preprocessing stage, we build the binary tree recursively. It is easy to see that the number of nodes in the tree is
O(n)
and the depth is
O(\log n)
. For a leaf node, we take
O(d^{2})
-time to compute the matrix
\alpha_{i}\cdot v_{i}v_{i}^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
. For an internal node, we take
O(d^{2})
-time to add up the matrices of its left and right children. Thus, the total preprocessing time is
O(nd^{2})
.

In the query stage, we walk along a path from the root to a leaf, which has
O(\log n)
steps. In each step, we compute the inner product between
A
and the current node’s matrix, which takes
O(d^{2})
-time. And we compute the inner product between
A
and its left child node’s matrix, which also takes
O(d^{2})
-time. Then, we toss a coin and decide which subtree to move. Hence, each query takes
O(d^{2}\log n)
-time.

The theorem is then proved.
∎

Lemma 7.6

(Running time of Procedure
RandBSS+
in Algorithm
3
)
.

Algorithm
3
runs in

•

O(|D|d^{2})
-time for preprocessing,

•

O(d^{2}\log(|D|)+d^{\omega})
-time per iteration, and

•

O(\varepsilon^{-1}d)
iterations.

Thus, the total running time is,

\displaystyle O(|D|d^{2}+\varepsilon^{-1}d\cdot(d^{2}\log|D|+d^{\omega})).

Proof.

In each call of the Procedure
RandBSS+
in Algorithm
3
,

•

Finding orthonormal basis takes
O(|D|d^{2})
.

•

In the line
9
, it runs
O(|D|d^{2})
times.

•

The while loop repeat
O(\varepsilon^{-1}d)
times.

–

Line
14
is computing
(u_{j}I-B_{j})\in\mathbb{C}^{d\times d}
,
(u_{j}I-B_{j})^{-1}
. This part takes
O(d^{\omega})
time
14
14

14

Note that this step seems to be very difficult to speed up via the Sherman-Morrison formula since
u_{j}
changes in each iteration and the update is of high rank.
.

–

Note that line
15
of Procedure
RandBSS+
in Algorithm
3
runs
O(d^{2}\log|D|)
times.

So, the time complexity of Procedure
RandBSS+
in Algorithm
3
is

\displaystyle O(|D|d^{2}+\varepsilon^{-1}d\cdot(d^{2}\log|D|+d^{\omega})).

∎

Lemma 7.7

(Correctness of Procedure
RandBSS+
in Algorithm
3
)
.

Given any dimension
d
linear space
{\cal F}
, any distribution
D
over the domain of
{\cal F}
, and any
\varepsilon>0
,
\textsc{RandBSS+}(d,\mathcal{F},D,\varepsilon)
is an
\varepsilon
-WBSP that terminates in
O(d/\varepsilon)
rounds with probability
1-1/200
.

Proof.

We first claim that, for each
j\in[m]
,
{\sf x}_{j}
has the same distribution as
D_{j}
, where

\displaystyle D_{j}(x)=D(x)\cdot(v(x)^{\top}E_{j}v(x))/\Phi_{j}~~~\forall x\in D

Notice that sampling from distribution
D_{j}
can be reformulated as an Online Quadratic-Form Sampling Problem: the vectors are
\{v(x)\}_{x\in D}
, the coefficients are
\{D(x)\}_{x\in D}
, and the query matrix is
E^{\prime}_{j}:=E_{j}/\Phi_{j}
. Then, we have
D_{j}={\cal D}_{E^{\prime}_{j}}
defined in Problem
7.4
.
Hence, by Theorem
7.5
, we can use the data structure (Algorithm
4
) to efficiently sample from
D_{j}
.

Therefore, the sample
{\sf x}_{j}
in each iteration is generated from the same distribution as the original randomized BSS algorithm (Algorithm
2
). Then, the WBSP guarantee and the number of iterations immediately follow from the proof of
[
CP19a
, Lemma 5.1]
.

The proof of the lemma is then completed.
∎

Proof of Theorem
7.3
.

The running time of the algorithm follows from Lemma
7.6
, and the correctness follows from Lemma
7.7
.
∎

7.3
Trade-off between preprocessing and query

In this section, we consider the preprocessing and query trade-off in the data structure for quadratic form sampling problem. In the following theorem, we give a new data structure that takes less time in preprocessing and more time for each query than Theorem
7.5
, and the space complexity is also reduced from
O(nd^{2})
to
O(nd)
.

Theorem 7.8
.

There is a data structure (Algorithms
5
and
6
) that uses
O(nd)
spaces for the Online Quadratic-Form Sampling Problem with the following procedures:

•

Init
(n,d,\{v_{1},\dots,v_{n}\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d},\{\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}\}\subset\mathbb{R})
: the data structure preprocesses in time
O(nd^{\omega-1})
.

•

Query
(A\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d})
: Given a PSD matrix
A
, the
Query
operation samples
i\in[n]
exactly from the probability distribution
{\cal D}_{A}
defined in Problem
7.4
in
O(d^{2}\log(n/d)+d^{\omega})
-time.

Proof.

The time and space complexities follow from Lemma
7.9
. And the correctness follows from Lemma
7.10
.
∎
