\displaystyle(1-\varepsilon)\cdot\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}|x^{*}(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t\leq\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}|x^{*}(t)\cdot H(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t\leq\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}|x^{*}(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t.

(72)

By Lemma
12.29
, we know that
(x^{*}\cdot H)*G^{(j)}_{\sigma,b}
always satisfies Property II (in Definition
12.27
):

\displaystyle~T\|x^{*}(t)H(t)*G_{\sigma,b}^{(j)}(t)\|^{2}_{T}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\int_{0}^{T}|x^{*}(t)H(t)*G_{\sigma,b}^{(j)}(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle~(1-\varepsilon_{0})\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}|x^{*}(t)H(t)*G_{\sigma,b}^{(j)}(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~(1-\varepsilon_{0})\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}|(\widehat{x}^{*}(f)*\widehat{H}(f))*\widehat{G}_{\sigma,b}^{(j)}(f)|^{2}\mathrm{d}f

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~(1-\varepsilon_{0})(\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}|(\widehat{x}^{*}(f)*\widehat{H}(f))\cdot\widehat{G}_{\sigma,b}^{(j)}(f)|^{2}\mathrm{d}f+\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}|(\widehat{x}^{*}(f)*\widehat{H}(f))\cdot\widehat{G}_{\sigma,b}^{(j)}(f)|^{2}\mathrm{d}f)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle~(1-\varepsilon_{0})\cdot\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}|(\widehat{x}^{*}(f)*\widehat{H}(f))\cdot\widehat{G}_{\sigma,b}^{(j)}(f)|^{2}\mathrm{d}f

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle~(1-\varepsilon_{0})\cdot\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}|(\widehat{x}^{*}(f)*\widehat{H}(f))|^{2}\mathrm{d}f

(73)

where the first step follows from the definition of the norm, the second step is from Lemma
12.29
, the third step is due to Parseval’s Theorem, the forth step is based on the Large Offset event not happening, the fifth step is based on simple algebra, the last step is because of Lemma
12.29
.

We also have that

\displaystyle~T\|x_{S_{f}}(t)-x_{S}(t)\|_{T}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~T\|x_{\mathcal{T}}\|_{T}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~(T/(1-\varepsilon)^{2})\cdot\|x_{\mathcal{T}}(t)\cdot H(t)\|^{2}_{T}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\frac{1}{1-\varepsilon^{2}}\cdot\int_{0}^{T}|x_{\mathcal{T}}(t)\cdot H(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\frac{1}{1-\varepsilon^{2}}\cdot\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|x_{\mathcal{T}}(t)\cdot H(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\frac{1}{1-\varepsilon^{2}}\cdot\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|\widehat{x}_{\mathcal{T}}(f)*\widehat{H}(f)|^{2}\mathrm{d}f

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\frac{1}{1-\varepsilon^{2}}\cdot\sum_{j=1}^{B}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|\widehat{x}_{\mathcal{T}_{j}}(f)*\widehat{H}(f)|^{2}\mathrm{d}f

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\frac{k^{2}}{(1-\varepsilon)^{2}(k-\delta)^{2}}\cdot\sum_{j\in B\setminus S_{g}}T\|(x^{*}(t)\cdot H(t))*G^{(j)}_{\sigma,b}(t))\|^{2}_{T}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\frac{k^{2}}{(1-c\varepsilon)(1-\varepsilon)^{2}(k-\delta)^{2}}\cdot\sum_{j\in B\setminus S_{g}}T\|(g(t)\cdot H(t))*G^{(j)}_{\sigma,b}(t))\|^{2}_{T}

(74)

where the first step follows from Definition of
\mathcal{T}
, the second step follows from Eq. (
72
), the third step is based on definition of norm, the forth step follows from simple algebra, the fifth step follows from Parseval’s Theorem, the six step is due to Large Offset event not happening, the seventh step is due to Lemma
12.29
, the eighth step follows from Eq. (
70
).

In the following, we have that

\displaystyle~\sum_{j\in[B]}T\cdot\|(g(t)\cdot H(t))*G^{(j)}_{\sigma,b}(t)\|^{2}_{T}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\sum_{j\in[B]}\int_{0}^{T}|(g^{*}(t)\cdot H(t))*G^{(j)}_{\sigma,b}(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\sum_{j\in[B]}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|(g^{*}(t)\cdot H(t))*G^{(j)}_{\sigma,b}(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\sum_{j\in[B]}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|(\widehat{g}(f)*\widehat{H}(f))\cdot\widehat{G}_{\sigma,b}^{(j)}(f)|^{2}\mathrm{d}f

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\frac{k^{2}}{(k-\delta)^{2}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|\widehat{g}(f)*\widehat{H}(f)|^{2}\mathrm{d}f

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\frac{k^{2}}{(k-\delta)^{2}}\cdot\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|g(t)\cdot H(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\frac{k^{2}}{(k-\delta)^{2}}\cdot\int_{0}^{T}|g(t)\cdot H(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\frac{k^{2}}{(k-\delta)^{2}}\cdot\int_{0}^{T}|g(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~T\frac{k^{2}}{(k-\delta)^{2}}\|g(t)\|^{2}_{T}

(75)

where the first step is due to the definition of norm, the second step follows from
g(t)=0
when
t\notin[0,T]
, the third step follows from Parseval’s Theorem, the forth step is because of Lemma
12.29
, the fifth step is from Parseval’s Theorem, the sixth step is based on
g(t)=0
when
t\notin[0,T]
, the seventh step is from
|H(t)|^{2}\leq 1
, the last step is from the definition of norm.
We have that

\displaystyle~T\|x_{S_{f}}(t)-x_{S}(t)\|_{T}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\frac{k^{2}}{(1-c\varepsilon)(1-\varepsilon)^{2}(k-\delta)^{2}}\cdot\sum_{j\in B\setminus S_{g}}T\|(g^{*}(t)\cdot H(t))*G^{(j)}_{\sigma,b}(t))\|^{2}_{T}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\frac{k^{2}}{(1-c\varepsilon)(1-\varepsilon)^{2}(k-\delta)^{2}}\sum_{j\in[B]}T\|(g^{*}(t)\cdot H(t))*G^{(j)}_{\sigma,b}(t))\|^{2}_{T}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\frac{k^{4}}{(1-c\varepsilon)(1-\varepsilon)^{2}(k-\delta)^{4}}T\|g(t)\|^{2}_{T}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~(1+O(\varepsilon))T\|g(t)\|^{2}_{T}

where the first step follows from Eq. (
12.9
), the second step follows from simple algebra, the third step is due to Eq.(
12.9
), the forth step is because of the reason that
\delta
is much smaller than
\varepsilon
and
\varepsilon<1
.
∎

Lemma 12.40

(High SNR signal’s energy)
.

Let
x^{*}(t)=\sum_{j=1}^{k}v_{j}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}t}
and
x(t)=x^{*}(t)+g(t)
be our observable signal. Let
\mathcal{N}_{1}^{2}:=\varepsilon_{1}(\|g(t)\|_{T}^{2}+\delta\|x^{*}(t)\|_{T}^{2})
. Let
C_{1},\cdots,C_{l}
are the
\mathcal{N}_{1}
-heavy clusters from Definition
12.11
. Let
S^{*}
denotes the set of frequencies
f^{*}\in\{f_{j}\}_{j\in[k]}
such that,
f^{*}\in C_{i}
for some
i\in[l]
, and

\displaystyle\int_{C_{i}}|\widehat{x^{*}\cdot H}(f)|^{2}\mathrm{d}f\geq T\mathcal{N}_{1}^{2}/k,

Let
S
denotes the set of frequencies
f^{*}\in S^{*}
such that,
f^{*}\in C_{j}
for some
j\in[l]
, and

\displaystyle\int_{C_{j}}|\widehat{x\cdot H}(f)|^{2}\mathrm{d}f\geq\varepsilon_{2}T\mathcal{N}_{1}^{2}/k,

Let
S_{f}
be defined in Definition
12.31
. Then, we have that,

\displaystyle\|x_{S_{f}}\|_{T}\leq(1+O(\varepsilon))\|g\|_{T}+\|x^{*}\|_{T}

Proof.

We have that,

\displaystyle\|x_{S_{f}}\|_{T}\leq

\displaystyle~\|x_{S_{f}}-x_{S}\|_{T}+\|x_{S^{*}}-x^{*}\|_{T}+\|x_{S}-x_{S^{*}}\|_{T}+\|x^{*}\|_{T}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle~\|x_{S_{f}}-x_{S}\|_{T}+\|x_{S^{*}}-x^{*}\|_{T}+\|x-x_{S^{*}}\|_{T}+\|x^{*}\|_{T}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle~\|x_{S_{f}}-x_{S}\|_{T}+\|x_{S^{*}}-x^{*}\|_{T}+\|x-x^{*}\|_{T}+\|x^{*}\|_{T}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\|x_{S_{f}}-x_{S}\|_{T}+\|g\|_{T}+\|x^{*}\|_{T}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~(1+O(\varepsilon))\|g\|_{T}+\|x^{*}\|_{T},

where the first step follows from triangle inequality, the second step follows from Corollary
12.22
, the third step follows from triangle inequality, the forth step follows from Claim
12.12
, where the last step follows from Lemma
12.39
.
∎

12.10
(3+\sqrt{2}+\varepsilon)
-approximate algorithm

In this section, we prove the main result: a
(3+\sqrt{2}+\varepsilon)
-approximate Fourier interpolation algorithm, which significantly improves the accuracy of
[
CKPS16
]
’s result.

Theorem 12.41

(Fourier interpolation with
(3+\sqrt{2}+\varepsilon)
-approximation error)
.

Let
x(t)=x^{*}(t)+g(t)
, where
x^{*}
is
k
-Fourier-sparse signal with frequencies in
[-F,F]
. Given samples of
x
over
[0,T]
we can output
y(t)
such that with probability at least
1-2^{-\Omega(k)}
,

\|y-x^{*}\|_{T}\leq(3+\sqrt{2}+\varepsilon)\|g\|_{T}+\delta\|x^{*}\|_{T}.

Our algorithm uses
\mathrm{poly}(k,\varepsilon^{-1},\log(1/\delta))\log(FT)

samples and
\mathrm{poly}(k,\varepsilon^{-1},\log(1/\delta))\cdot\log^{2}(FT)
time. The output
y

is
\mathrm{poly}(k,\varepsilon^{-1},\log(1/\delta))
-Fourier-sparse signal.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{N}_{2}^{2}:=\varepsilon_{1}\varepsilon_{2}(\|g(t)\|_{T}^{2}+\delta\|x^{*}(t)\|_{T}^{2})
,
\mathcal{N}_{1}^{2}:=\varepsilon_{1}(\|g(t)\|_{T}^{2}+\delta\|x^{*}(t)\|_{T}^{2})
be the heavy cluster parameter.

First, by Lemma
12.12
, there is a set of frequencies
S^{*}\subset[k]
and
x_{S^{*}}(t)=\underset{j\in S^{*}}{\sum}v_{j}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}t}
such that

\displaystyle\|x_{S^{*}}-x^{*}\|_{T}^{2}\leq(1+\varepsilon)\mathcal{N}_{1}^{2}.

(76)

Furthermore, each
f_{j}
with
j\in S^{*}
belongs to an
\mathcal{N}_{1}
-heavy cluster
C_{j}
with respect to the filter function
H
defined in Definition
12.6
.

By Definition
12.11
of heavy cluster, it holds that

\displaystyle\int_{C_{j}}|\widehat{H\cdot x^{*}}(f)|^{2}\mathrm{d}f\geq T\mathcal{N}_{1}^{2}/k.

By Definition
12.11
, we also have
|C_{j}|\leq k\cdot\Delta_{h}
, where
\Delta_{h}
is the bandwidth of
\widehat{H}
.

Let
\Delta\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, and
\Delta>k\cdot\Delta_{h}
, which implies that
C_{j}\subseteq[f_{j}-\Delta,f_{j}+\Delta]
. Thus, we have

\displaystyle\int_{f_{j}-\Delta}^{f_{j}+\Delta}|\widehat{H\cdot x^{*}}(f)|^{2}\mathrm{d}f\geq T\mathcal{N}_{1}^{2}/k.

By Corollary
12.22
, there is a set of frequencies
S\subset S^{*}
and
x_{S}(t)=\underset{j\in S}{\sum}v_{j}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}t}
such that

\displaystyle\|x_{S}-x_{S^{*}}\|_{T}^{2}\leq(1+O(\sqrt{\varepsilon_{2}}))\|x-x_{S^{*}}\|_{T}^{2}.

Let
g^{\prime}=x-x_{S^{*}}
.

In the following part, we will only focus on recovering the high SNR frequency.
Let
S_{f}
be defined in Definition
12.31
. It’s to know
S_{f}\subset S

By applying Theorem
12.36
, there is an algorithm that outputs a set of frequencies
L\subset\mathbb{R}
such that,
|L|=O(k/(\varepsilon_{0}\varepsilon_{1}\varepsilon_{2}))
,
and with probability at least
1-2^{-\Omega(k)}
, for any
f_{j}
with
j\in S_{f}
, there is a
\widetilde{f}\in L
such that,

\displaystyle|f_{j}-\widetilde{f}|\lesssim\Delta\sqrt{\Delta T}.

We define a map
p:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow L
as follows:

\displaystyle p(f):=\arg\min_{\widetilde{f}\in L}~|f-\widetilde{f}|~~~\forall f\in\mathbb{R}.

Then,
x_{S}(t)
can be expressed as

\displaystyle x_{S_{f}}(t)=

\displaystyle~\sum_{j\in S_{f}}v_{j}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}t}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\sum_{j\in S_{f}}v_{j}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\cdot p(f_{j})t}\cdot e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\cdot(f_{j}-p(f_{j}))t}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\sum_{\widetilde{f}\in L}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\widetilde{f}t}\cdot\sum_{j\in S_{f}:~p(f_{j})=\widetilde{f}}v_{j}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}(f_{j}-\widetilde{f})t},

where the first step follows from the definition of
x_{S}(t)
, the last step follows from interchanging the summations.

For each
\widetilde{f}_{i}\in L
, by Corollary
12.2
with
x^{*}=x_{S},\Delta=\Delta\sqrt{\Delta T}
, we have that there exist degree
d=O(T\Delta\sqrt{\Delta T}+k^{3}\log k+k\log 1/\delta)
polynomials
P_{i}(t)
corresponding to
\widetilde{f}_{i}\in L
such that,

\displaystyle\|x_{S_{f}}(t)-\sum_{\widetilde{f}_{i}\in L}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\widetilde{f}_{i}t}P_{i}(t)\|_{T}\leq\sqrt{\delta}\|x_{S_{f}}(t)\|_{T}

(77)

Define the following function family:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}:=\mathrm{span}\Big\{e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\widetilde{f}t}\cdot t^{j}~{|}~\forall\widetilde{f}\in L,j\in\{0,1,\dots,d\}\Big\}.

Note that
\sum_{\widetilde{f}_{i}\in L}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\widetilde{f}_{i}t}P_{i}(t)\in{\cal F}
.

By Claim
12.16
, for function family
\cal F
,
K_{\mathrm{Uniform[0,T]}}=O((|L|d)^{4}\log^{3}(|L|d))
.

By Lemma
12.18
, we have that, choosing a set
W
of
O(\varepsilon^{-1}K_{\mathrm{Uniform[0,T]}}\log(|L|d/\rho))

i.i.d. samples uniformly at random over duration
[0,T]
is a
(\varepsilon,\rho)
-WBSP.

By Lemma
12.19
, there is an algorithm that runs in
O(\varepsilon^{-1}|W|(|L|d)^{\omega-1}\log(1/\rho))
-time using samples in
W
, and outputs
y^{\prime}(t)\in{\cal F}
such that, with probability
1-\rho
,

\displaystyle\Big\|y^{\prime}(t)-\sum_{\widetilde{f}_{i}\in L}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\widetilde{f}_{i}t}P_{i}(t)\Big\|_{T}\leq(1+\varepsilon)\Big\|x(t)-\sum_{\widetilde{f}_{i}\in L}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\widetilde{f}_{i}t}P_{i}(t)\Big\|_{T}

(78)

Then by Lemma
12.3
, we have that there is a

(kd)
-Fourier-sparse signal
y(t)
, such that

\displaystyle\|y-y^{\prime}\|_{T}\leq\delta^{\prime}

(79)

where
\delta^{\prime}>0
is any positive real number, thus,
y
can be arbitrarily close to
y^{\prime}
.

Moreover, the sparsity of
y(t)
is

\displaystyle kd=kO(T\Delta\sqrt{\Delta T}+k^{3}\log k+k\log 1/\delta)=\mathrm{poly}(k,\varepsilon^{-1},\log(1/\delta)).

Therefore, the total approximation error can be upper bounded as follows:

\displaystyle~\|y-x^{*}\|_{T}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\|y-y^{\prime}\|_{T}+\Big\|y^{\prime}-\sum_{\widetilde{f}_{i}\in L}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\widetilde{f}_{i}t}P_{i}(t)\Big\|_{T}+\Big\|\sum_{\widetilde{f}_{i}\in L}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\widetilde{f}_{i}t}P_{i}(t)-x^{*}\Big\|_{T}

(Triangle inequality)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~(1+0.1\varepsilon)\Big\|y^{\prime}-\sum_{\widetilde{f}_{i}\in L}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\widetilde{f}_{i}t}P_{i}(t)\Big\|_{T}+\Big\|\sum_{\widetilde{f}_{i}\in L}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\widetilde{f}_{i}t}P_{i}(t)-x^{*}\Big\|_{T}

(Eq. (
79
))

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~(1+2\varepsilon)\Big\|x-\sum_{\widetilde{f}_{i}\in L}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\widetilde{f}_{i}t}P_{i}(t)\Big\|_{T}+\Big\|\sum_{\widetilde{f}_{i}\in L}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\widetilde{f}_{i}t}P_{i}(t)-x^{*}\Big\|_{T}

(Eq. (
78
))

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~(1+2\varepsilon)(\|x-x_{S_{f}}\|_{T}+\|x_{S_{f}}-x^{*}\|_{T})+2(1+2\varepsilon)\|\sum_{\widetilde{f}_{i}\in L}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\widetilde{f}_{i}t}P_{i}(t)-x_{S_{f}}\|_{T}

(Triangle Inequality)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~(1+2\varepsilon)(\|x-x_{S}\|_{T}+2\|x_{S_{f}}-x_{S}\|_{T}+\|x_{S}-x^{*}\|_{T})+2(1+2\varepsilon)\|\sum_{\widetilde{f}_{i}\in L}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\widetilde{f}_{i}t}P_{i}(t)-x_{S_{f}}\|_{T}

(Triangle Inequality)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~(1+2\varepsilon)(\|x-x_{S}\|_{T}+\|x_{S}-x^{*}\|_{T})+O(\sqrt{\delta})\|x_{S_{f}}(t)\|_{T}+2(1+2\varepsilon)\|x_{S_{f}}-x_{S}\|_{T}

(Eq. (
77
))

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~(1+2\varepsilon)(1+\sqrt{2}+O(\sqrt{\varepsilon}))\|g\|_{T}+O(\sqrt{\delta})\|x^{*}\|_{T}+O(\sqrt{\delta})\|x_{S_{f}}(t)\|_{T}+2(1+2\varepsilon)\|x_{S_{f}}-x_{S}\|_{T}

(Lemma
12.37
)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~(1+2\varepsilon)(1+\sqrt{2}+O(\sqrt{\varepsilon}))\|g\|_{T}+O(\sqrt{\delta})\|x^{*}\|_{T}+O(\sqrt{\delta})(\|g\|_{T}+\|x^{*}\|_{T})+2(1+2\varepsilon)(1+O(\varepsilon))\|g(t)\|_{T}

(Lemma
12.38
)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~(3+\sqrt{2}+O(\sqrt{\varepsilon}))\|g\|_{T}+O(\sqrt{\delta})\|x^{*}\|_{T}

By re-scaling
\varepsilon
and
\delta
, we prove the theorem.

∎

Appendix

Appendix A
High Dimensional Reduction Under Noiseless Assumption

This section is organized as follows:

•

Section
A.1
shows that Fourier basis is linear independent.

•

Section
A.2
shows that, there is straightforward algorithm for signal estimation under noiseless setting.

A.1
Fourier basis is linear independent on randomly sampled points

Lemma A.1
.

Given a basis
\mathcal{B}
of
m
known vectors
b_{1},b_{2},\cdots b_{m}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, let
\Lambda(\mathcal{B})\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
denote the lattice

\displaystyle\Lambda(\mathcal{B})=\Big\{z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:z=\sum_{i=1}^{m}c_{i}b_{i},c_{i}\in\mathbb{Z},\forall i\in[m]\Big\}

Suppose that
f_{1},f_{2},\cdots,f_{k}\in\Lambda(\mathcal{B})
.
Randomly samples a vector
o\sim{\cal N}(0,I_{d})
. Let
t=k^{-1}\cdot(T/2+o\cdot{\min}({\max}_{j\in[k]}|\langle f_{j},o\rangle|^{-1},k^{-1}T))
. Let
t_{i}:=(i-1)\cdot t
for
i\in[k]
.

Let
v_{j}=(\exp(2\pi\mathbf{i}\langle f_{j},t_{1}\rangle),\exp(2\pi\mathbf{i}\langle f_{j},t_{2}\rangle),\cdots,\exp(2\pi\mathbf{i}\langle f_{j},t_{k}\rangle))
for
j\in[k]
. We have that
v_{1},v_{2},\cdots,v_{k}
are linear independent with probability
1
.

Proof.

We have that

\displaystyle u_{j}:=\begin{bmatrix}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\langle f_{1},0\cdot t\rangle}\\
e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\langle f_{1},1\cdot t\rangle}\\
\vdots\\
e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\langle f_{1},(k-1)\cdot t\rangle}\\
\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}1\\
e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\langle f_{1},t\rangle}\\
\vdots\\
(e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\langle f_{1},t\rangle})^{k-1}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}1\\
w_{1}\\
\vdots\\
w_{1}^{k-1}\end{bmatrix},

where
w_{1}:=e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\langle f_{1},t\rangle}
. Similarly, we can define
w_{j}:=e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\langle f_{j},t\rangle}
. And we have

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}|&|&&|\\
u_{1}&u_{2}&\cdots&u_{k}\\
|&|&&|\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}1&1&\cdots&1\\
w_{1}&w_{2}&\cdots&w_{k}\\
w_{1}^{2}&w_{2}^{2}&\cdots&w_{k}^{2}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
w_{1}^{k-1}&w_{2}^{k-1}&\cdots&w_{k}^{k-1}\end{bmatrix},

which is a Vandermonde matrix. Hence, they are linearly independent as long as
w_{1},\dots,w_{k}
are distinct. Or equivalently,

\displaystyle\langle f_{1},t\rangle\mod{2\pi},\cdots,\langle f_{k},t\rangle\mod{2\pi}

are distinct.

Next, we will show that
w_{1},\dots,w_{k}
are distinct with probability
1
. We first show that:

\displaystyle\langle f_{i},t\rangle\leq\langle f_{i},o\rangle/{\max}_{j\in[k]}|\langle f_{j},o\rangle|\in[-1,1]\subset[-\pi,\pi].

Then,
\langle f_{i},t\rangle=\langle f_{j},t\rangle\mod{2\pi}
is equivalent to
\langle f_{i},t\rangle=\langle f_{j},t\rangle
, and is equivalent to
\langle f_{i}-f_{j},t\rangle=0
, and is equivalent to
\langle f_{i}-f_{j},o\rangle=0
. However,
\langle f_{i}-f_{j},o\rangle
follows from
{\cal N}(0,\|f_{i}-f_{j}\|_{2}^{2})
. By our assumption,
\|f_{i}-f_{j}\|_{2}^{2}\neq 0
. Thus,

\displaystyle\Pr_{t\sim{\cal N}(0,I_{d})}[\langle f_{i}-f_{j},t\rangle=0]=0.

Therefore, by union bound,

\displaystyle\Pr_{t\sim{\cal N}(0,I_{d})}[\exists i\neq j\in[k]:\langle f_{i}-f_{j},t\rangle=0]=0.

∎

A.2
Reduction

Lemma A.2

(High Dimension Noiseless)
.

Given a basis
\mathcal{B}
of
m
known vectors
b_{1},b_{2},\cdots b_{m}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, let
\Lambda(\mathcal{B})\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
denote the lattice

\displaystyle\Lambda(\mathcal{B})=\Big\{z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:z=\sum_{i=1}^{m}c_{i}b_{i},c_{i}\in\mathbb{Z},\forall i\in[m]\Big\}

Suppose that
f_{1},f_{2},\cdots,f_{k}\in\Lambda(\mathcal{B})
. Let
x^{*}(t)=\sum_{j=1}^{k}v_{j}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\langle f_{j},t\rangle}
. Given
x^{*}(t)
is observable for
t\in[0,T]^{d}
. Let
\eta=\min_{i\neq j}\|f_{j}-f_{i}\|_{\infty}
.

Given
D,\eta\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
. Suppose that there is an algorithm
\textsc{FrequencyEstimation}(x^{*},k,\rho,d,F,T,\mathcal{B})
that

•

takes
\mathcal{S}(k,\rho,d,F,T,\eta)
samples,

•

runs
\mathcal{T}(k,\rho,d,F,T,\eta)
time,

•

output a set
L
of frequencies such that, for each
f_{i}
, there is
f^{\prime}_{i}\in L
,
|f_{i}-f^{\prime}_{i}|\leq D/T
, holds with probability
1-\rho
.

Let
C=|L|\cdot(D/T+\sqrt{m}\|{\cal B}\|)^{m}\cdot\frac{\pi^{m/2}}{(m/2)!}\cdot\frac{1}{|\det({\cal B})|}
. If
C\leq d
, then, there is an algorithm (
\textsc{SignalEstimation}(x,k,d,F,T,\mathcal{B})
) that

•

takes
O(\widetilde{k}+\mathcal{S}(k,d,F,T,\eta))
samples,

•

runs
O(\widetilde{k}^{\omega}+\widetilde{k}^{2}d+\mathcal{T}(k,d,F,T,\eta))
time,

•

output
y(t)=\sum_{j=1}^{\widetilde{k}}v_{j}^{\prime}\cdot\exp(2\pi\mathbf{i}\langle f_{j}^{\prime},t\rangle)
such that

–

\widetilde{k}\leq C
,

–

y(t)=x(t)
, holds with probability
(1-\rho)^{2}
.

Proof.

First, we recover the frequencies by utilizing the algorithm
\textsc{FrequencyEstimation}(x,k,d,T,F,\mathcal{B})
. Let
L
be the set of frequencies output by the algorithm
\textsc{FrequencyEstimation}(x,k,d,T,F,\mathcal{B})
.

We define
\widetilde{L}
as follows:

\displaystyle\widetilde{L}:=\{f\in\Lambda(\mathcal{B})~|~\exists f^{\prime}\in L,~|f^{\prime}-f|<D/T\}.

We use
\widetilde{k}
to denote the size of set
\widetilde{L}
.
We use
f^{\prime}_{1},f^{\prime}_{2},\cdots,f^{\prime}_{\widetilde{k}}
to denote the frequencies in the set
\widetilde{L}
.

By applying Lemma
10.2
, we have that

\displaystyle\widetilde{k}=|\widetilde{L}|\leq|L|\cdot(D/T+\sqrt{m}\|{\cal B}\|)^{m}\cdot\frac{\pi^{m/2}}{(m/2)!}\cdot\frac{1}{|\det({\cal B})|}.

Next, we focus on recovering magnitude
v^{\prime}\in\mathbb{C}^{\widetilde{k}}
.
Let
\rho_{1}:=\rho
. First, we randomly samples a vector
o\sim{\cal N}(0,I_{d})
. Let
t=k^{-1}\cdot(T/2+o\cdot{\min}({\max}_{j\in[k]}|\langle f_{j},o\rangle|^{-1},\rho_{1}T))
. Let
t_{i}:=(i-1)\cdot t
for
i\in[k]
. We have that
|o\cdot{\min}({\max}_{j\in[k]}|\langle f_{j},o\rangle|^{-1},\rho_{1}T)|\leq o\rho_{1}T
. Then with probability
1-\rho_{1}
,
|o|\leq 1/(2\rho_{1})
, then
t_{i}\in[0,T]^{d},\forall i\in[k]
.

Consider the matrix
A\in\mathbb{C}^{\widetilde{k}\times\widetilde{k}}
, where the
(i,j)
-th entry in
A
is
A_{i,j}=\exp(2\pi\mathbf{i}\langle f^{\prime}_{j},t_{i}\rangle)
for each
i\in[\widetilde{k}]
and
j\in[\widetilde{k}]
. Let
b=(x^{*}(t_{1}),x^{*}(t_{2}),\cdots,x^{*}(t_{\widetilde{k}}))^{\top}\in\mathbb{C}^{\widetilde{k}}
.

Then, if the following linear system solvable, then we solve the following linear system:

\displaystyle Av^{\prime}=b.

(80)

We output
y(t)=\sum_{j=1}^{\widetilde{k}}v_{j}^{\prime}\cdot\exp(2\pi\mathbf{i}\langle f_{j}^{\prime},t\rangle)
, and we have that
y(t)=x^{*}(t)
.

Finally, by Lemma
A.1
, we have that Eq. (
80
) is solvable with a large probability.

∎

Appendix B
Semi-continuous Approximation

In this section, we justify the usefulness of the semi-continuous setting by showing that for any
k
-Fourier-sparse, it can be approximated by a
k
-Fourier-sparse semi-continuous signal. This section is organized as follows:

•

In Sections
B.1
and
B.2
, we give some technical tools on the Gaussian multiplier.

•

In Section
B.3
, we prove the main result of this section (Theorem
B.8
), which is incomparable to the existence result in
[
CKPS16
]
.

•

In Section
B.4
, we give a fast optimal-sparsity Fourier interpolation algorithm with a different error guarantee.

•

In Section
B.5
, we also show that the frequency gap of the approximation can be increased to
\Theta(1/T)
if we slightly blow up the sparsity of the approximation signal (Corollary
B.12
).

B.1
Properties related to Gaussians

Definition B.1

(Gaussian Multiplier)
.

For parameters
\mu,\sigma
, we define

\displaystyle M_{\mu,\sigma^{2}}(x)=e^{-\frac{(x-\mu)^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}}

i.e. it is a Gaussian scaled so that its maximum value is
1
.

We stat a standard result for Gaussian multiplier (see
[
LLM21
]
for example).

Lemma B.2
.

Let
0<\varepsilon<0.1
be a parameter. Let
c
be a real number such that
0<c\leq(\log(1/\varepsilon))^{-1/2}
. Let
M
be defined as in Definition
B.1
. Define

\displaystyle f(x):=\sum_{j=-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{c}{\sqrt{2\pi}}M_{cj\sigma,\sigma^{2}}(x)\,.

Then

\displaystyle 1-\varepsilon^{10}\leq f(x)\leq 1+\varepsilon^{10}

for all
x
. Furthermore, let
\alpha\leq 1
be a parameter and
c=\alpha(\log(1/\varepsilon))^{-1/2}
. Then

\displaystyle 1-\varepsilon^{10/\alpha^{2}}\leq f(x)\leq 1+\varepsilon^{10/\alpha^{2}}

for all
x
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality
\sigma=1
. Now the function
f
is
c
-periodic and even, so we may consider its Fourier expansion

\displaystyle f(x)=

\displaystyle~a_{0}+2\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}a_{j}\cos(2j\pi x/c)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~a_{0}+2a_{1}\cos\left(\frac{2\pi x}{c}\right)+2a_{2}\cos\left(\frac{4\pi x}{c}\right)+\dots

and we will now compute the Fourier coefficients. First note that

\displaystyle a_{0}=

\displaystyle~\frac{1}{c}\int_{0}^{c}f(x)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\sum_{j=-\infty}^{\infty}\int_{c(j+1)}^{cj}M_{0,1}(x)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~1.

where the second step follows from the definition of
f
.

Next, for any
j\geq 1
,

\displaystyle a_{j}=

\displaystyle~\frac{1}{c}\int_{0}^{c}f(x)\cos\left(\frac{2\pi jx}{c}\right)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\frac{1}{c}\int_{0}^{c}\sum_{j=-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{c}{\sqrt{2\pi}}M_{cj,1}(x)\cos\left(\frac{2\pi jx}{c}\right)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\sum_{l=-\infty}^{\infty}\int_{c(l+1)}^{cl}M_{0,1}(x)\cos\left(\frac{2\pi jx}{c}\right)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\sum_{l=-\infty}^{\infty}\int_{c(l+1)}^{cl}\exp(-\frac{x^{2}}{2})\cos\left(\frac{2\pi jx}{c}\right)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2}\left(\exp(\frac{-x^{2}}{2}+\frac{2\pi\mathbf{i}jx}{c})+\exp(\frac{-x^{2}}{2}-\frac{2\pi\mathbf{i}jx}{c})\right)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\exp(-\frac{2\pi^{2}j^{2}}{c^{2}})\,.

where the second step follows from the definition of
f
. The fourth step follows from the definition of
M
.

Then we can claim that

\displaystyle|f(x)-1|\leq

\displaystyle~2\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\exp(-\frac{2\pi^{2}j^{2}}{c^{2}})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~2\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\exp(-15j/c^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\frac{2\exp(-15/c^{2})}{1-\exp(-15/c^{2})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\frac{2\varepsilon^{15/\alpha^{2}}}{1-\varepsilon^{15}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\varepsilon^{10/\alpha^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\varepsilon^{10}

where the first step follows from
a_{0}=1
, the forth step follows from
\alpha\leq 1
and
c=\alpha(\log(1/\varepsilon))^{-1/2}
, the fifth step follows from
\varepsilon\in(0,0.1)
,
∎

Claim B.3
.

Let
\varepsilon\in(0,0.01)
be a parameter. Let
\varepsilon_{0}=2\varepsilon
,
c=0.01/\sqrt{\log(1/\varepsilon)}
,
K=\lceil\frac{1+0.5\varepsilon}{c\varepsilon^{2}}\rceil
. Let
M:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be defined as in Definition
B.1
. Define
f:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}

\displaystyle f(x)=\sum_{j=-K}^{K}\frac{c}{\sqrt{2\pi}}M_{\mu_{j},\sigma^{2}}(x),\text{~~~where~~~}\mu_{j}={cj\varepsilon^{2}l},~~~\sigma^{2}=\varepsilon^{4}l^{2}\,.

Then the following properties is satisfied

•

Part 1.
f(x)\in[0,1+\varepsilon_{0}]
, for all
x

•

Part 2.
f(x)\in[1-\varepsilon_{0},1+\varepsilon_{0}]
, for all
x\in[-l,l]

•

Part 3.
f(x)\in[0,\varepsilon_{0}]
, for all
|x|\geq(1+\varepsilon)l

Proof.

Using Lemma
B.2
with setting the following parameter choice
\sigma=\varepsilon^{2}l
,
c=0.01(\log(1/\varepsilon))^{-1/2}
, we define

\displaystyle f_{0}(x)=\sum_{j=-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{c}{\sqrt{2\pi}}M_{\mu_{j},\sigma^{2}}\,.

we can get that
1-\varepsilon\leq f_{0}(x)\leq 1+\varepsilon
.

We will then upper bound the perturbation between
f
and
f_{0}
. Firstly, we will provide a upper bound for
M
when
x/(\varepsilon^{2}l)-cj\geq 10

\displaystyle\frac{c}{\sqrt{2\pi}}M_{\mu_{j},\sigma^{2}}(x)=

\displaystyle~\frac{c}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\exp(-(x-cj\varepsilon^{2}l)^{2}/(2\varepsilon^{4}l^{2}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\frac{c}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\exp(-(x/(\varepsilon^{2}l)-cj)^{2}/2)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\exp(-|x/(\varepsilon^{2}l)-cj|)

(81)

where the first step follows from the definition of
M
, the third step follows from
x/(\varepsilon^{2}l)-cj\geq 10
.

Next we will claim that

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\exp(-cj)=

\displaystyle~\frac{1}{1-\exp(-c)}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\frac{2}{c}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\frac{200}{\varepsilon}

(82)

where the first step follows from
c>0
and the sum of geometric sequence, the second step follows from
1-\exp(-x)>x/2
when
x<0.1
, the third step follows from
c\geq 0.01\varepsilon
.

Because of the symmetry of
M
, We will also claim that

\displaystyle M_{\mu,\sigma^{2}}(x)=M_{-\mu,\sigma^{2}}(-x)

(83)

Part 1.

Because
M_{\mu,\sigma}=\exp(-(x-\mu)^{2}/\sigma^{2})\geq 0
, we can obtain that
f(x)\geq 0
. Besides, we will claim that

\displaystyle f(x)\leq

\displaystyle~f_{0}(x)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~1+\varepsilon

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\varepsilon_{0}

where the first step follows from
M_{\mu,\sigma}\geq 0
.

Part 2.

Firstly, we upper bound
f
when
x\in[-l,l]
. To bound
\sum_{j=K+1}^{\infty}\frac{c}{\sqrt{2\pi}}M_{\mu_{j},\sigma^{2}}(x)
, we obtain

\displaystyle cj-x/(\varepsilon^{2}l)\geq

\displaystyle~cj-1/(\varepsilon^{2})

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle~c(K+1)-1/\varepsilon^{2}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle~0.5/\varepsilon

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle~10

(84)

where the first step follows from
x\in[-l,l]
, the second step follows from
j\geq K+1
, the third step follows from
K+1\geq(1+0.5\varepsilon)/(c\varepsilon^{2})
.

Then we can upper bound

\displaystyle\sum_{j=K+1}^{\infty}\frac{c}{\sqrt{2\pi}}M_{\mu_{j},\sigma^{2}}(x)\leq

\displaystyle~\sum_{j=K+1}^{\infty}\exp(x/(\varepsilon^{2}l)-cj)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\frac{200}{\varepsilon}\exp(x/(\varepsilon^{2}l)-c(K+1))

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\frac{200}{\varepsilon}\exp(-0.5/\varepsilon)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~0.1\varepsilon

(85)

where the first step follows from Eq. (
81
) and Eq. (
84
), the second step follows from Eq. (
82
), the third step follows from Eq. (
84
), the last step follows from
\varepsilon\in(0,0.01)
.

We conclude

\displaystyle|f(x)-1|=

\displaystyle~\Big|f_{0}(x)-1-\sum_{j=K+1}^{\infty}\frac{c}{\sqrt{2\pi}}M_{\mu_{j},\sigma^{2}}(x)-\sum_{j=-\infty}^{-K-1}\frac{c}{\sqrt{2\pi}}M_{\mu_{j},\sigma^{2}}(x)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~|f_{0}(x)-1|+\Big|\sum_{j=K+1}^{\infty}\frac{c}{\sqrt{2\pi}}M_{\mu_{j},\sigma^{2}}(x)\Big|+\Big|\sum_{j=-\infty}^{-K-1}\frac{c}{\sqrt{2\pi}}M_{\mu_{j},\sigma^{2}}(x)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\varepsilon+0.1\varepsilon+0.1\varepsilon

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\varepsilon_{0}

where the first step follows from the definition of
f
and
f_{0}
, the second step follows from the triangle inequality, the third step follows from
|f_{0}(x)-1|\leq\varepsilon
, Eq.(
85
) and Eq. (
83
).

Part 3.

Secondly, we will provide the upper bound for
f
when
|x|>(1+\varepsilon)l
. Without loss of generality, we can only consider
x>(1+\varepsilon)l
because of Eq. (
83
). To bound
\sum_{j=-K}^{K}\frac{c}{\sqrt{2\pi}}M_{\mu_{j},\sigma^{2}}(x)
. We obtain

\displaystyle x/(\varepsilon^{2}l)-cj\geq

\displaystyle~x/(\varepsilon^{2}l)-cK

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle~(1+\varepsilon)/\varepsilon^{2}-cK

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle~0.25/\varepsilon

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle~10

(86)

where the first step follows from
j\leq K
, the second step follows from
x>(1+\varepsilon)l
, the third step follows from
K\leq(1+0.5\varepsilon)/(c\varepsilon^{2})+1
and
c\leq 0.01\leq 0.25/\varepsilon
.

Then we can upper bound

\displaystyle\sum_{j=-K}^{K}\frac{c}{\sqrt{2\pi}}M_{\mu_{j},\sigma^{2}}(x)\leq

\displaystyle~\sum_{j=-K}^{K}\exp(cj-x/(\varepsilon^{2}l))

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\frac{200}{\varepsilon}\exp(cK-x/(\varepsilon^{2}l))

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\frac{200}{\varepsilon}\exp(-0.25/\varepsilon)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\varepsilon

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\varepsilon_{0}

(87)

where the first step follows from Eq. (
81
) and Eq. (
86
), the second step follows from Eq. (
82
), the third step follows from Eq. (
86
), the fourth step follows from
\varepsilon\in(0,0.01)
.

∎

Lemma B.4
.

Given
c\in\mathbb{R},~\gamma,l,\sigma\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
,
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
. Let
M:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be defined as in Definition
B.1
. For
|a-b|\leq\gamma
, We have that

\displaystyle|{{M}_{\mu,\sigma^{2}}(a)\exp(c\mathbf{i}a)}-{{M}_{\mu,\sigma^{2}}(b)\exp(c\mathbf{i}b)}|\leq({\sigma}^{-1}+|c|)\gamma.

Proof.

We have that

\displaystyle~|{M}_{\mu,\sigma^{2}}(b)-{{M}_{\mu,\sigma^{2}}(a)}|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~|\exp(-(b-\mu)^{2}/(2\sigma^{2}))-\exp(-(a-\mu)^{2}/(2\sigma^{2}))|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~|b-a||(\xi-\mu)\exp(-(\xi-\mu)^{2}/(2\sigma^{2}))/(\sigma^{2})|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\gamma/\sigma

(88)

where the first step follows from the definition of
M
, the second step defines
\xi\in[a,b]
and follows from Lagrange’s Mean Value Theorem, the last step follows from
|x\exp(-x^{2}/2)|\leq 1
.
We have that

\displaystyle~|{{M}_{\mu,\sigma^{2}}(a)\exp(c\mathbf{i}a)}-{{M}_{\mu,\sigma^{2}}(b)\exp(c\mathbf{i}b)}|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~|{{M}_{\mu,\sigma^{2}}(a)\exp(c\mathbf{i}a)}-{{M}_{\mu,\sigma^{2}}(b)\exp(c\mathbf{i}a)}|+|{{M}_{\mu,\sigma^{2}}(b)\exp(c\mathbf{i}a)}-{{M}_{\mu,\sigma^{2}}(b)\exp(c\mathbf{i}b)}|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~|{M}_{\mu,\sigma^{2}}(a)-{M}_{\mu,\sigma^{2}}(b)|+{M}_{\mu,\sigma^{2}}(b)\cdot|{\exp(c\mathbf{i}a)}-{\exp(c\mathbf{i}b)}|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\frac{\gamma}{\sigma}+{M}_{\mu,\sigma^{2}}(b)\cdot|c(a-b)|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\frac{\gamma}{\sigma}+|c|\gamma

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~(\frac{1}{\sigma}+|c|)\gamma

where the first follows from triangle inequality, the second follows from
|\exp(\mathbf{i}\theta)|=1
, the third step follows from Eq. (
88
) and that arc length is greater than chord length, the fourth step follows from
M\leq 1
and the assume in the statement.

∎

B.2
Continuous Fourier transform

B.2.1
Bounding the tails

The goal of this section is to prove Lemma
B.5
,

Lemma B.5
.

Let
\mu\in\mathbb{R},\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}_{+},\sigma
,
F,F_{0},\varepsilon_{1}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
. Let
\sigma\in(0,1)
,
\varepsilon_{1}\in(0,0.1)
,
F>1
. Let
M:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be defined as in Definition
B.1
. Let
x:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
be a function.
Let
\mathrm{supp}(\widehat{x})\subseteq[-F,F]
.
If

\displaystyle F_{0}>{\sigma}^{-1}\cdot\log^{1/2}(4F/\varepsilon_{1})+F,

then we have that,

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}(\widehat{M}_{\mu,\sigma^{2}}(f)*\widehat{x}(f))^{\alpha}\mathrm{d}f-\int_{-F_{0}}^{F_{0}}(\widehat{M}_{\mu,\sigma^{2}}(f)*\widehat{x}(f))^{\alpha}\mathrm{d}f\Big|\leq

\displaystyle~\|\widehat{x}\|^{\alpha}_{\infty}\varepsilon_{1}/{F^{\alpha}}.

Proof.

First, we will calculate
\widehat{M}
. Then, we provide a bound on the tail of
\widehat{M}
. Finally, we conclude our proof.

We can claim that

\displaystyle\widehat{M}_{\mu,\sigma^{2}}(f)=\sqrt{2\pi\sigma^{2}}\cdot\exp(-2\pi\mathbf{i}\mu f)\cdot M_{0,1/(4\pi^{2}\sigma^{2})}.

Taking the
|\cdot|
on both sides of the above equation, we get

\displaystyle|\widehat{M}_{\mu,\sigma^{2}}(f)|=\sqrt{2\pi\sigma^{2}}\cdot|M_{0,1/(4\pi^{2}\sigma^{2})}|

We start with

\displaystyle\int_{F_{0}-F}^{\infty}|\widehat{M}_{\mu,\sigma^{2}}(f)|^{\alpha}\mathrm{d}f=

\displaystyle~\sqrt{2\pi\sigma^{2}}\cdot\int_{F_{0}-F}^{\infty}|M_{0,1/(4\pi^{2}\sigma^{2})}|^{\alpha}\mathrm{d}f

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\sqrt{2\pi\sigma^{2}}\cdot\int_{F_{0}-F}^{\infty}\exp(-2\alpha\pi^{2}\sigma^{2}f^{2})\mathrm{d}f

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\frac{1}{\sqrt{\alpha\pi}}\cdot\int_{\sqrt{2\alpha}\pi\sigma(F_{0}-F)}^{\infty}\exp(-\xi^{2})\mathrm{d}\xi

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\frac{1}{\sqrt{\alpha\pi}}\cdot\int_{\sqrt{2\alpha}\pi\sigma(F_{0}-F)}^{\infty}\exp(-\sqrt{2\alpha}\pi\sigma(F_{0}-F)\xi)\mathrm{d}\xi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\frac{1}{\sqrt{\alpha\pi}}\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\alpha}\pi\sigma(F_{0}-F)}\exp(-2\alpha\pi^{2}\sigma^{2}(F_{0}-F)^{2})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\varepsilon_{1}/(4F)^{2\alpha}.

(89)

where the second step follows definition of
M
, the third step follows from
\xi=\sqrt{2\alpha}\pi\sigma f
, the forth step follows from
\xi\leq\sqrt{2\alpha}\pi\sigma(F_{0}-F)
, the last step follows from lower bound on
F_{0}
in the Lemma statement.

Finally, we can bound LHS in the statement as follows

\displaystyle~\Big|\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}(\widehat{M}_{\mu,\sigma^{2}}(f)*\widehat{x}(f))^{\alpha}\mathrm{d}f-\int_{-F_{0}}^{F_{0}}(\widehat{M}_{\mu,\sigma^{2}}(f)*\widehat{x}(f))^{\alpha}\mathrm{d}f\Big|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\Big|\int_{-\infty}^{F_{0}}(\widehat{M}_{\mu,\sigma^{2}}(f)*\widehat{x}(f))^{\alpha}\mathrm{d}f+\int_{F_{0}}^{\infty}(\widehat{M}_{\mu,\sigma^{2}}(f)*\widehat{x}(f))^{\alpha}\mathrm{d}f\Big|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\Big|\int_{-\infty}^{F_{0}}(\int_{-F}^{F}\widehat{M}_{\mu,\sigma^{2}}(f-\xi)\cdot\widehat{x}(\xi)\mathrm{d}\xi)^{\alpha}\mathrm{d}f+\int_{F_{0}}^{\infty}(\int_{-F}^{F}\widehat{M}_{\mu,\sigma^{2}}(f-\xi)\cdot\widehat{x}(\xi)\mathrm{d}\xi)^{\alpha}\mathrm{d}f\Big|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\Big|\int_{F_{0}}^{\infty}(\int_{-F}^{F}\widehat{M}_{\mu,\sigma^{2}}(f-\xi)\cdot\widehat{x}(\xi)\mathrm{d}\xi)^{\alpha}\mathrm{d}f\Big|+\Big|\int_{-\infty}^{-F_{0}}(\int_{-F}^{F}\widehat{M}_{\mu,\sigma^{2}}(f-\xi)\cdot\widehat{x}(\xi)\mathrm{d}\xi)^{\alpha}\mathrm{d}f\Big|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\int_{F_{0}}^{\infty}(\int_{-F}^{F}|\widehat{M}_{\mu,\sigma^{2}}(f-\xi)\cdot\widehat{x}(\xi)|\mathrm{d}\xi)^{\alpha}\mathrm{d}f+\int_{-\infty}^{-F_{0}}(\int_{-F}^{F}|\widehat{M}_{\mu,\sigma^{2}}(f-\xi)\cdot\widehat{x}(\xi)|\mathrm{d}\xi)^{\alpha}\mathrm{d}f

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\|\widehat{x}\|^{\alpha}_{\infty}\cdot(\int_{F_{0}}^{\infty}(\int_{-F}^{F}|\widehat{M}_{\mu,\sigma^{2}}(f-\xi)|\mathrm{d}\xi)^{\alpha}\mathrm{d}f+\int_{-\infty}^{-F_{0}}(\int_{-F}^{F}|\widehat{M}_{\mu,\sigma^{2}}(f-\xi)|\mathrm{d}\xi)^{\alpha}\mathrm{d}f)

(90)

where the third step follows from triangle inequality, the fourth step follows from triangle inequality.

For the second term in the above Eq. (
B.2.1
)

\displaystyle\int_{F_{0}}^{\infty}(\int_{-F}^{F}|\widehat{M}_{\mu,\sigma^{2}}(f-\xi)|\mathrm{d}\xi)^{\alpha}\mathrm{d}f\leq

\displaystyle~\int_{F_{0}}^{\infty}(\int_{-F}^{F}|\widehat{M}_{\mu,\sigma^{2}}(f-F)|\mathrm{d}\xi)^{\alpha}\mathrm{d}f

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\int_{F_{0}}^{\infty}|2F\widehat{M}_{\mu,\sigma^{2}}(f-F)|^{\alpha}\mathrm{d}f

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~(2F)^{\alpha}\cdot\int_{F_{0}}^{\infty}|\widehat{M}_{\mu,\sigma^{2}}(f-F)|^{\alpha}\mathrm{d}f

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~(2F)^{\alpha}\cdot\int_{F_{0}-F}^{\infty}|\widehat{M}_{\mu,\sigma^{2}}(\xi)|^{\alpha}\mathrm{d}\xi

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~(2F)^{\alpha}\cdot\varepsilon_{1}/(4F)^{2\alpha}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\varepsilon_{1}/(2F^{\alpha})

(91)

where the first step follows from what
\widehat{M}_{\mu,\sigma^{2}}(f-\xi)\leq\widehat{M}_{\mu,\sigma^{2}}(f-F)
because
f-\xi\geq f-F\geq F_{0}-F>0
, the forth step follows from
f-F=\xi
, second last step follows from Eq. (
B.2.1
).

Similarly, we have that

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{-F_{0}}(\int_{-F}^{F}|\widehat{M}_{\mu,\sigma^{2}}(f-\xi)|\mathrm{d}\xi)^{\alpha}\mathrm{d}f\leq\varepsilon_{1}/2.

(92)

Combining Eq. (
B.2.1
), (
B.2.1
) and (
92
) completes the proof.
∎

B.2.2
Bounding the convolution

The goal of this section is to prove Lemma
B.6
.

Lemma B.6
.

Given
\mu^{\prime},F,\sigma^{\prime},\gamma\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
. Let
M:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be defined as in Definition
B.1
. Let
x:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
be a function.
For simplicity, let
M=M_{\mu^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime 2}}
.
We have that for
\forall f\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle|\widehat{M}*\widehat{x}(f)-\sum_{\mu\in\mathbb{Z}\gamma}\int_{\mu-\gamma/2}^{\mu+\gamma/2}\widehat{x}(\xi)\mathrm{d}\xi\cdot\widehat{M}(f)*\delta(f-\mu)|\leq(\sigma^{\prime 2}+\sigma^{\prime}|\mu^{\prime}|)\gamma^{2}\cdot\|\widehat{x}\|_{1}.

Proof.

We will separate
\widehat{M}*\widehat{x}(f)
into
\widehat{M}(f)*(\widehat{x}(f)\cdot\mathrm{rect}_{\gamma/2}(f-\mu))
where
\mu\in\gamma\mathbb{Z}
. This decomposes
\widehat{x}
into different intervals. Then, we get a bound for each interval.

We can rewrite
\widehat{M}_{\mu^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime 2}}(f)
in the following sense,

\displaystyle\widehat{M}_{\mu^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime 2}}(f)=

\displaystyle~\sqrt{2\pi\sigma^{\prime 2}}\cdot\exp(-2\pi^{2}\sigma^{\prime 2}f^{2})\cdot\exp(-2\pi\mathbf{i}\mu^{\prime}f)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\sqrt{2\pi\sigma^{\prime 2}}\cdot M_{0,1/(4\pi^{2}\sigma^{\prime 2})}(f)\cdot\exp(-2\pi\mathbf{i}\mu^{\prime}f)

where the second step follows from the definition of
M
.

First, we consider the first term in the LHS of our statement

\displaystyle\widehat{M}(f)*(\widehat{x}(f)\cdot\mathrm{rect}_{\gamma/2}(f-\mu))=

\displaystyle~\widehat{M}(f)*\Big(\int_{\mu-\gamma/2}^{\mu+\gamma/2}\widehat{x}(\xi)\delta(f-\xi)\mathrm{d}\xi\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\int_{\mu-\gamma/2}^{\mu+\gamma/2}\widehat{x}(\xi)\cdot\widehat{M}(f)*\delta(f-\xi)\mathrm{d}\xi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\int_{\mu-\gamma/2}^{\mu+\gamma/2}\widehat{x}(\xi)\cdot\widehat{M}(f-\xi)\mathrm{d}\xi

(93)

where the first step follows from definition of
\mathrm{rect}
function, the last step follows from
\widehat{M}(f)*\delta(f-\xi)=\widehat{M}(f-\xi)
.

Now, we consider the second term in the LHS of our statement

\displaystyle\int_{\mu-\gamma/2}^{\mu+\gamma/2}\widehat{x}(\xi)\mathrm{d}\xi\cdot\widehat{M}(f)*\delta(f-\mu)=

\displaystyle~\int_{\mu-\gamma/2}^{\mu+\gamma/2}\widehat{x}(\xi)\cdot\widehat{M}(f)*\delta(f-\mu)\mathrm{d}\xi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\int_{\mu-\gamma/2}^{\mu+\gamma/2}\widehat{x}(\xi)\cdot\widehat{M}(f-\mu)\mathrm{d}\xi.

(94)

where the last step follows from
\widehat{M}(f)*\delta(f-\mu)=\widehat{M}(f-\mu)
.

By Lemma
B.4
(with
\gamma=\gamma/2
,
\sigma=1/(2\pi\sigma^{\prime})
,
\mu=0
,
c=-2\pi\mu^{\prime}
), we have that

\displaystyle~|\widehat{M}(f-\xi)-\widehat{M}(f-\mu)|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\sqrt{2\pi\sigma^{\prime 2}}|M_{0,1/(4\pi^{2}\sigma^{\prime 2})}(f-\xi)\exp(-2\pi\mathbf{i}\mu^{\prime}(f-\xi))-M_{0,1/(4\pi^{2}\sigma^{\prime 2})}(f-\mu)\exp(-2\pi\mathbf{i}\mu^{\prime}(f-\mu))|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\sqrt{2\pi\sigma^{\prime 2}}(2\pi\sigma^{\prime}+2\pi|\mu^{\prime}|)\gamma

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle~(\sigma^{\prime 2}+\sigma^{\prime}|\mu^{\prime}|)\gamma

(95)

where the first step from the calculation of
\widehat{M}
, the second step follows from Lemma
B.4
.

Thus, we can claim that
\forall f\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle~|\widehat{M}(f)*(\widehat{x}(f)\cdot\mathrm{rect}_{\gamma/2}(f-\mu))-\int_{\mu-\gamma/2}^{\mu+\gamma/2}\widehat{x}(f)\mathrm{d}f\cdot\widehat{M}(f)*\delta(f-\mu)|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~|\int_{\mu-\gamma/2}^{\mu+\gamma/2}\widehat{x}(\xi)\cdot\widehat{M}(f-\xi)\mathrm{d}\xi-\int_{\mu-\gamma/2}^{\mu+\gamma/2}\widehat{x}(\xi)\cdot\widehat{M}(f-\mu)\mathrm{d}\xi|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~|\int_{\mu-\gamma/2}^{\mu+\gamma/2}\widehat{x}(\xi)\cdot(\widehat{M}(f-\xi)-\widehat{M}(f-\mu))\mathrm{d}\xi|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\int_{\mu-\gamma/2}^{\mu+\gamma/2}|\widehat{x}(\xi)|\cdot|\widehat{M}(f-\xi)-\widehat{M}(f-\mu)|\mathrm{d}\xi

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\underset{\xi\in[\mu-\gamma/2,\mu+\gamma/2]}{\max}\{|\widehat{M}(f-\xi)-\widehat{M}(f-\mu)|\}\cdot\int_{\mu-\gamma/2}^{\mu+\gamma/2}|\widehat{x}(\xi)|\mathrm{d}\xi

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle~(\sigma^{\prime 2}+\sigma^{\prime}|\mu^{\prime}|)\gamma\cdot\int_{\mu-\gamma/2}^{\mu+\gamma/2}|\widehat{x}(\xi)|\mathrm{d}\xi.

(96)

where the first step follows from from Eq. (
93
), Eq. (
94
), the third step follows from triangle inequality, and the last step follows from Eq. (
95
).

As a result, we have that for
f

\displaystyle~|\widehat{M}*\widehat{x}(f)-\sum_{\mu\in\mathbb{Z}\gamma}\int_{\mu-\gamma/2}^{\mu+\gamma/2}\widehat{x}(\xi)\mathrm{d}\xi\cdot\widehat{M}(f)*\delta(f-\mu)|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~|\sum_{\mu\in\mathbb{Z}\gamma}\widehat{M}(f)*(\widehat{x}(f)\cdot\mathrm{rect}_{\gamma/2}(f-\mu))-\sum_{\mu\in\mathbb{Z}\gamma}\int_{\mu-\gamma/2}^{\mu+\gamma/2}\widehat{x}(\xi)\mathrm{d}\xi\cdot\widehat{M}(f)*\delta(f-\mu)|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\sum_{\mu\in\mathbb{Z}\gamma}|\widehat{M}(f)*(\widehat{x}(f)\cdot\mathrm{rect}_{\gamma/2}(f-\mu))-\int_{\mu-\gamma/2}^{\mu+\gamma/2}\widehat{x}(f)\mathrm{d}f\cdot\widehat{M}(f)*\delta(f-\mu)|

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle~(\sigma^{\prime 2}+\sigma^{\prime}|\mu^{\prime}|)\gamma\cdot\sum_{\mu\in\mathbb{Z}\gamma}\int_{\mu-\gamma/2}^{\mu+\gamma/2}|\widehat{x}(\xi)|\mathrm{d}\xi

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~(\sigma^{\prime 2}+\sigma^{\prime}|\mu^{\prime}|)\gamma\cdot\|\widehat{x}\|_{1}

where the first step follows from
\sum_{\mu\in\mathbb{Z}\gamma}\mathrm{rect}_{\gamma/2}(f-\mu)=1
, the second step follows from triangle inequality, the third step follows from Eq. (
96
), the last step follows from the definition of
\ell_{1}
norm.

Thus we complete proof.
∎

Choice of parameters

The following lemma shows how to take the parameters in this section.

Lemma B.7
.

Let
\varepsilon_{0},\varepsilon,F,c_{0},\sigma^{\prime},T,F_{0},K,\varepsilon_{1}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that

•

\varepsilon_{0}=0.01

•

\varepsilon_{1}\leq\varepsilon^{2}/T

•

\sigma^{\prime}=\sqrt{2}\varepsilon_{0}^{2}T

•

c_{0}=0.01/\sqrt{\log(1/\varepsilon_{0})}

•

K=\lceil\frac{1+0.5\varepsilon_{0}}{c_{0}\varepsilon_{0}^{2}}\rceil\leq 2/(c_{0}\varepsilon_{0}^{2})

•

F_{0}={\sigma^{\prime}}^{-1}\cdot\log^{1/2}(4F/\varepsilon_{1})+F

We have that

•

Part 1.

c_{0}K\lesssim 1

•

Part 2.

F_{0}\lesssim(1/T)\cdot\log^{1/2}(FT/\varepsilon)+F

Proof.

We will prove them separately.

Part 1.

We can show

\displaystyle c_{0}K=

\displaystyle~c_{0}\lceil\frac{1+0.5\varepsilon_{0}}{c_{0}\varepsilon_{0}^{2}}\rceil\leq~\frac{2c_{0}}{c_{0}\varepsilon_{0}^{2}}\lesssim~1.

where the first step follows from the definition of
K
, the third step follows from the definition of
\varepsilon_{0}
.

Part 2.

We have that

\displaystyle F_{0}=

\displaystyle~{\sigma^{\prime}}^{-1}\cdot\log^{1/2}(4F/\varepsilon_{1})+F

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle~1/(\varepsilon_{0}^{2}T)\cdot\log^{1/2}(F/\varepsilon_{1})+F

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle~1/T\cdot\log^{1/2}(F/\varepsilon_{1})+F

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle~1/T\cdot\log^{1/2}(FT/\varepsilon^{2})+F

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle~1/T\cdot\log^{1/2}(FT/\varepsilon)+F

where the second step follows from the definition of
\sigma^{\prime}
, the third step follows from the definition of
\varepsilon_{0}
, the fourth step follows from the definition of
\varepsilon_{1}\geq\varepsilon^{2}/T
.

∎

B.3
Semi-continuouse approximation of Fourier-sparse signals

The main theorem of this section is stated and proved below.

Theorem B.8

(Sparse Signal is Semi-continuous)
.

Given
\gamma,\varepsilon\in(0,0.1),F,T\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
. Let
x:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
be a function that such that
x
is
k
-Fourier-sparse
and
\mathrm{supp}(\widehat{x})\subseteq[-F,F]
.
Let
\gamma>0
and
\varepsilon_{1}>0
.
Then there is an algorithm output a
k^{\prime}
-Fourier-sparse signal (
k^{\prime}\leq k
),

\displaystyle x^{\prime}(t)=\sum_{i=1}^{k^{\prime}}v_{i}\exp(2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{i}t)

such that

\displaystyle\|x^{\prime}-x\|_{T}^{2}\lesssim(F_{0}T^{3}\gamma^{2}+\varepsilon_{1}/(F^{2}T))\cdot\|\widehat{x}\|_{1}^{2}

where
\gamma=\underset{i\neq j}{\min}|f_{i}-f_{j}|
,
f_{i}\in[-F,F],~f_{i}\in\gamma\mathbb{Z},~\forall i\in[k^{\prime}]
and
F_{0}=\Omega(T^{-1}\log^{1/2}(F/\varepsilon_{1})+F)
.

Further, if
\varepsilon_{1}\leq\varepsilon^{2}/T
and
\gamma\leq\varepsilon/\sqrt{F_{0}T^{3}}
, then we have

\displaystyle\|x^{\prime}-x\|_{T}^{2}\lesssim\varepsilon^{2}\|\widehat{x}\|_{1}^{2}

Proof.

First, we will introduce our choice for
x^{\prime}
. Then, we bound
|\widehat{M}_{j}*\widehat{x}-\widehat{M}_{j}*\widehat{x}^{\prime}|
. Finally, show the relationship between
|\widehat{M}_{j}*\widehat{x}-\widehat{M}_{j}*\widehat{x}^{\prime}|
and the LHS in our statement by utilizing
M
to bridge the integral calculated in the time domain
\|x^{\prime}-x\|_{T}
and the integral calculated in the frequency domain
\|\widehat{x}\|_{1}
.

Let
x^{\prime}
be chosen as

\displaystyle x^{\prime}(t)=\sum_{\mu\in\mathbb{Z}\gamma}\int_{\mu-\gamma/2}^{\mu+\gamma/2}\widehat{x}(f)\mathrm{d}f\cdot\exp(2\pi\mathbf{i}\mu t).

Then,

\displaystyle\|\widehat{x^{\prime}}\|_{1}=\sum_{\mu\in\mathbb{Z}\gamma}\int_{\mu-\gamma/2}^{\mu+\gamma/2}\widehat{x}(f)\mathrm{d}f=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\widehat{x}(f)\mathrm{d}f=\|\widehat{x}\|_{1}.

(97)

Let
c_{0},\varepsilon_{0}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
,
K\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
such that
\varepsilon_{0}=0.01\in(0,0.1),~c_{0}=0.01/\sqrt{\log(1/\varepsilon_{0})},~K=\lceil\frac{1+0.5\varepsilon_{0}}{c_{0}\varepsilon_{0}^{2}}\rceil\leq 2/(c_{0}\varepsilon_{0}^{2})
.

For simplicity, let
M_{j}(t)=M_{\mu_{j}^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime 2}}(t)=M_{c_{0}j\varepsilon_{0}^{2}T,2\varepsilon_{0}^{4}T^{2}}(t)
, then

\displaystyle\widehat{M}_{j}(f)=\sqrt{2\pi\sigma^{\prime 2}}\exp(-2\pi^{2}\sigma^{\prime 2}f^{2})\exp(-2\pi\mathbf{i}\mu^{\prime}_{j}f).

and

\displaystyle\mu^{\prime}_{j}\leq

\displaystyle~c_{0}K\varepsilon_{0}^{2}T

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~c_{0}(\frac{2}{c_{0}\varepsilon_{0}^{2}})\varepsilon_{0}^{2}T

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~2T.

where the second step follows from upper bound on
K
.

We have that

\displaystyle\widehat{x}^{\prime}(f)=\sum_{\mu\in\mathbb{Z}\gamma}\int_{\mu-\gamma/2}^{\mu+\gamma/2}\widehat{x}(f)\mathrm{d}f\cdot\delta(f-\mu).

So, we can convolute
\widehat{M}
at the both sides and get

\displaystyle\widehat{M}_{j}(f)*\widehat{x}^{\prime}(f)=\sum_{\mu\in\mathbb{Z}\gamma}\int_{\mu-\gamma/2}^{\mu+\gamma/2}\widehat{x}(f)\mathrm{d}f\cdot\widehat{M}_{j}(f)*\delta(f-\mu).

(98)

By Lemma
B.6
,
we have that for
\forall f\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle~|\widehat{M}_{j}*\widehat{x}-\widehat{M}_{j}*\widehat{x}^{\prime}|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\Big|\widehat{M}_{j}*\widehat{x}-\sum_{\mu\in\mathbb{Z}\gamma}\int_{\mu-\gamma/2}^{\mu+\gamma/2}\widehat{x}(f)\mathrm{d}f(\widehat{M}_{j}(f)*\delta(f-\mu))\Big|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~(\sigma^{\prime 2}+\sigma^{\prime}|\mu^{\prime}_{j}|)\gamma\cdot\|\widehat{x}\|_{1}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle~T^{2}\gamma\cdot\|\widehat{x}\|_{1}.

(99)

where the first step follows from Eq. (
98
), the second step follows from Lemma
B.6
, the third step follows the choice of
\mu^{\prime}_{j}
and
\sigma
and that
\varepsilon_{0}
is a constant.

Let
F_{0}
be defined as

\displaystyle F_{0}>{\sigma^{\prime}}^{-1}\cdot\log^{1/2}(4F/\varepsilon_{1})+F.

Next, we can bound
|x^{\prime}(t)-x(t)|^{2}
,

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}{M}_{j}^{2}(t)\cdot|x^{\prime}(t)-x(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t=

\displaystyle~\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|\widehat{M}_{j}*\widehat{x}^{\prime}(f)-\widehat{M}_{j}*\widehat{x}(f)|^{2}\mathrm{d}f

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\int_{-F_{0}}^{F_{0}}|\widehat{M}_{j}*\widehat{x}^{\prime}(f)-\widehat{M}_{j}*\widehat{x}(f)|^{2}\mathrm{d}f+\varepsilon_{1}\|\widehat{x}^{\prime}(f)-\widehat{x}(f)\|^{2}_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\int_{-F_{0}}^{F_{0}}|\widehat{M}_{j}*\widehat{x}^{\prime}(f)-\widehat{M}_{j}*\widehat{x}(f)|^{2}\mathrm{d}f+4\varepsilon_{1}\|\widehat{x}\|^{2}_{\infty}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle~2F_{0}T^{4}\gamma^{2}\cdot\|\widehat{x}\|_{1}^{2}+4\varepsilon_{1}\|\widehat{x}\|^{2}_{\infty}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle~\underbrace{(F_{0}T^{4}\gamma^{2}+\varepsilon_{1}/F^{2})\cdot\|\widehat{x}\|_{1}^{2}}_{\mathrm{err}}

(100)

where the second step follows from Lemma
B.5
, the third step follows from
\|\widehat{x}^{\prime}(f)-\widehat{x}(f)\|^{2}_{\infty}\leq(\|\widehat{x}^{\prime}(f)\|_{\infty}+\|\widehat{x}(f)\|_{\infty})^{2}=(2\|\widehat{x}(f)\|_{\infty})^{2}=4\|\widehat{x}\|^{2}_{\infty}
due to Eq. (
97
), the forth step follows from Eq. (
99
), the fifth step follows from
\|\widehat{x}\|_{\infty}\leq\|\widehat{x}\|_{1}
.

Then we can upper bound the LHS
\int_{0}^{T}|x^{\prime}(t)-x(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t
as follows:

\displaystyle~\int_{0}^{T}|x^{\prime}(t)-x(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\int_{0}^{T}\mathrm{rect}_{T}(t)|x^{\prime}(t)-x(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\int_{0}^{T}\sum_{j=-K}^{K}(\frac{c_{0}}{(1-2\varepsilon_{0})\sqrt{2\pi}}){M}_{j}^{2}(t)\cdot|x^{\prime}(t)-x(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\int_{0}^{T}\sum_{j=-K}^{K}c_{0}{M}_{j}^{2}(t)\cdot|x^{\prime}(t)-x(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~c_{0}\cdot\sum_{j=-K}^{K}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}{M}_{j}^{2}(t)\cdot|x^{\prime}(t)-x(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle~c_{0}K\cdot\mathrm{err}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle~\mathrm{err}

where the second step follows from Claim
B.3
and
{M}_{j}^{2}(t)={M}_{c_{0}j\varepsilon_{0}^{2}T,\varepsilon_{0}^{4}T^{2}}(t)
, the third step follows from
\varepsilon_{0}=0.01
, the forth step follows from relaxing integral range, the fifth step follows from Eq. (
100
), the last step follows from
c_{0}K\lesssim 1
due to Lemma
B.7
.

∎

B.4
Fast optimal-sparsity Fourier sparse recovery

Corollary B.9

(Our result)
.

For any
F>0,T>0,\varepsilon>0
. Let
x^{*}(t)=\sum_{j=1}^{k}v_{j}\exp({2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}t})
with
|f_{j}|\leq F
for
j\in[k]
. For observation
x(t)=x^{*}(t)+g(t)
, there exists an algorithm that takes

m=O(\varepsilon^{-1}k^{2}\log^{3}(k)\log(FT/(\delta\rho)))

random samples
t_{1},\dotsc,t_{m}\in[0,T]
, runs in
({\varepsilon}^{-1}FT)^{O(k)}
time, and outputs
y(t)=\sum_{j=1}^{k}\widetilde{v}_{j}\exp({2\pi\mathbf{i}\widetilde{f}_{j}t})
such that

\displaystyle\|y(t)-x^{*}(t)\|_{T}\leq(1+\varepsilon)\|g(t)\|_{T}+\delta\|\widehat{x}^{*}(f)\|_{1},

holds with probability
1-\rho
.

Proof.

Let
N_{f}=O(\frac{\delta}{T\sqrt{FT\log(1/\delta)}})\cdot\mathbb{Z}\cap[-F,F]
denote a net of frequencies. Because

\displaystyle\frac{\delta}{T\sqrt{F_{0}T}}\gtrsim

\displaystyle~\frac{\delta}{T\sqrt{\log^{1/2}(F/\varepsilon_{1})+FT}}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle~\frac{\delta}{T\sqrt{\log^{1/2}(FT/\delta^{2})+FT}}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle~\frac{\delta}{T\sqrt{\log(FT)+\log(1/\delta)+FT}}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle~\frac{\delta}{T\sqrt{FT\log(1/\delta)}}

where the first step follows from
F_{0}=\Omega(T^{-1}\log^{1/2}(F/\varepsilon_{1})+F)
, the second step follows from setting
\varepsilon_{1}=\delta^{2}/T
.

By Theorem
B.8
, for any signal
x^{*}(t)=\sum_{j=1}^{k}v_{j}\exp({2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}t})
, there exists a
k
-Fourier-sparse signal
\widetilde{x}(t)=\sum_{j=1}^{k}v^{\prime}_{j}\exp({2\pi\mathbf{i}f^{\prime}_{j}t})
such that,

\|x^{*}(t)-\widetilde{x}(t)\|_{T}\leq\delta\|\widehat{x}^{*}(f)\|_{1}

and
f^{\prime}_{1},\cdots,f^{\prime}_{k}\subseteq N_{f}
.

Because we have that

\displaystyle\|y(t)-x^{*}(t)\|_{T}\leq

\displaystyle~\|y(t)-\widetilde{x}(t)\|_{T}+\|\widetilde{x}(t)-x^{*}(t)\|_{T}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\|y(t)-\widetilde{x}(t)\|_{T}+\delta\|\widehat{x}^{*}(f)\|_{1}.

Let
S
be the set of i.i.d samples from
D(t)
of size
O(\varepsilon^{-1}k\log^{3}(k)\log(1/\rho_{0}))
,
w
be the corresponding weight in Algorithm
13
Procedure
SparseFT
line
3
. By Lemma
6.5
, we have that, for any
\cal F
, with probability at least
1-\rho_{0}
,

\displaystyle(1-\sqrt{\varepsilon})\|x\|_{T}\leq\|x\|_{S,w}\leq(1+\sqrt{\varepsilon})\|x\|_{T}.

In total, we enumerate
({\delta}^{-1}FT)^{O(k)}
function family
{\cal F}
in Algorithm
13
Procedure
SparseFT
line
6
. By taking
\rho_{0}=\rho{({\delta}(FT)^{-1})^{O(k)}}
, we have that the total success probability is at least

\displaystyle(1-\rho_{0})^{({\delta}^{-1}FT)^{O(k)}}\geq 1-{({\delta}^{-1}FT)^{O(k)}}\cdot\rho_{0}\geq 1-\rho

Thus, by Lemma
9.11
, with probability at least
1-\rho
, sampling
S
and
w
forms a
\varepsilon
-WBSP for every
\cal F
.

Finally, we bound
\|y(t)-\widetilde{x}(t)\|_{T}
as follows,

\displaystyle\|y(t)-\widetilde{x}(t)\|_{T}\leq

\displaystyle~(1+O(\varepsilon))\|x(t)-\widetilde{x}(t)\|_{T}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~(1+O(\varepsilon))(\|x(t)-x^{*}(t)\|_{T}+\|x^{*}(t)-\widetilde{x}(t)\|_{T})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~(1+O(\varepsilon))(\|g(t)\|_{T}+\delta\|\widehat{x}^{*}(f)\|_{1})

where the first step follows from the proof of Theorem
9.4
.

Combine the results above we have that

\displaystyle\|y(t)-x^{*}(t)\|_{T}\leq

\displaystyle~\|y(t)-\widetilde{x}(t)\|_{T}+\delta\|\widehat{x}^{*}(f)\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~(1+O(\varepsilon))\|g(t)\|_{T}+O(\delta)\|\widehat{x}^{*}(f)\|_{1}.

Note that our sample complexity is
|S|=O(\varepsilon^{-1}k\log^{3}(k)\log(1/\rho_{0}))=O(\varepsilon^{-1}k^{2}\log^{3}(k)\log(FT/(\delta\rho)))
.

∎

Algorithm 13

Recover
k
-sparse FT

1:

procedure

SparseFT
(
x,k,F,T,\varepsilon,\delta,\rho
)
\triangleright
Corollary
B.9

2:

m\leftarrow O(\varepsilon^{-1}k^{2}\log^{3}(k)\log(FT/(\delta\rho)))

3:

S,w\leftarrow\textsc{WeightedSketch}(m,k,T)
\triangleright
Algorithm
7

4:

We observe the signal
x(t)
for each
t\in S

5:

N_{f}\leftarrow O(\frac{\varepsilon}{T\sqrt{FT\log(1/\varepsilon)}})\cdot\mathbb{Z}\cap[-F,F]

6:

for

\{f^{\prime}_{1},\dotsc,f^{\prime}_{k}\}\in\binom{N_{f}}{[k]}

do

7:

Let
{\cal F}={\text{span}\{\exp({2\pi\mathbf{i}f^{\prime}_{1}t}),\cdots,\exp({2\pi\mathbf{i}f^{\prime}_{k}t})\}}

8:

h(t)\leftarrow{\mathrm{argmin}}_{h\in{\cal F}}\|h(t)-x(t)\|_{S,w}

9:

if

\|h(t)-x(t)\|_{S,w}\leq\|\widetilde{f}(t)-x(t)\|_{S,w}

then

10:

\widetilde{f}(t)\leftarrow h(t)

11:

if

12:

end

for

13:

\widetilde{f}(t)

14:

end

procedure

Lemma B.10

(Running time of Lemma
B.9
)
.

Procedure
SparseFT
in Algorithm
13
runs in
O(({\delta}^{-1}{FT})^{O(k)}\log(1/\rho))
times.

Proof.

In each call of the Procedure
SparseFT
in Algorithm
13
,

•

In the for loop, it repeats the line
8
for
({\delta}^{-1}{\log^{0.5}(1/\delta)(FT)^{1.5}})^{k}
times.

•

Note that each
8
of Procedure
SparseFT
in Algorithm
13
is solving linear regression. This part takes
O(\varepsilon^{-1}k^{\omega+1}\log^{3}(k)\log(FT/(\delta\rho)))
time.

So, the time complexity of Procedure
SparseFT
in Algorithm
13
is

\displaystyle O(({\delta}^{-1}{\log^{0.5}(1/\delta)(FT)^{1.5}})^{k})\cdot O(\varepsilon^{-1}k^{\omega+1}\log^{3}(k)\log(FT/(\delta\rho)))=O(({\varepsilon}^{-1}{FT})^{O(k)}\log(1/\rho)).

∎

B.5
Semi-continuous approximation with a constant frequency gap

In this section, we show that the semi-continuous approximation result in previous section can be further improved in terms of the frequency gap.

We first consider the one-sparse case in the following lemma.

Lemma B.11
.

Let
0<\delta<0.1
be a parameter. Let
x^{*}=v^{*}\exp(2\pi\mathbf{i}f^{*}t)
be a function such that
f^{*}\in[-c/T,c/T]
. Then, there exists
k>\log(1/\delta)
,
F\lesssim k/T
,
f_{1},\dots,f_{k}\in c/T\mathbb{Z}\cap[-F,F]
, and
v_{1},\dots,v_{k}\in\mathbb{C}

and for the function

\displaystyle\widetilde{x}(t)=\sum_{j=1}^{k}v_{j}\exp(2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}t)

we have that

\displaystyle\|x^{*}-\widetilde{x}\|_{T}\lesssim\delta|v^{*}|

Proof.

Note that
x^{*}(t)
can be written as

\displaystyle x^{*}(t)=\int_{-F}^{F}\widehat{x}^{*}(f)\exp(2\pi\mathbf{i}ft)\mathrm{d}f.

Now consider the Taylor expansion of

\displaystyle\exp(2\pi\mathbf{i}ft)=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{(2\pi\mathbf{i}ft)^{j}}{j!}

Note that since
f\in[-F,F]
,
t\in[0,T]
,
FT\lesssim k
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{j=k+1}^{\infty}\lvert\frac{(2\pi\mathbf{i}ft)^{j}}{j!}\rvert\leq

\displaystyle~\sum_{j=k+1}^{\infty}\lvert\frac{(2e\pi\mathbf{i}ft)^{j}}{j^{j}}\rvert

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\sum_{j=k+1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\exp(j)}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\frac{1}{\exp(k)}\cdot\frac{1}{1-1/e}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle~\delta_{1}

(101)

where the first step follows from Stirling’s formula
n!\geq\sqrt{2\pi}n^{n+0.5}\exp(-n)
, the second step follows from
2e^{2}\pi f\geq j
, the last step follows from
k\geq\log(1/\delta_{1})
.

In particular, if we define the approximator of exponential function

\displaystyle g_{f}(x)=\sum_{j=0}^{k}\frac{(2\pi\mathbf{i}ft)^{j}}{j!}

then over the interval
t\in[0,T]
,
f\in[-F,F]
,
FT\lesssim k
,

\displaystyle|\exp(2\pi\mathbf{i}ft)-g_{f}(t)|=

\displaystyle~|\sum_{j=k+1}^{\infty}\frac{(2\pi\mathbf{i}ft)^{j}}{j!}|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\sum_{j=k+1}^{\infty}\lvert\frac{(2\pi\mathbf{i}ft)^{j}}{j!}\rvert

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\delta_{1}.

(102)

where the first step follows from the definition of
g_{f}(x)
, the second step follows from the triangle inequality, the last step follows from Eq. (
101
).

Next, let
{\cal V}_{k}(f)=(1,f,\cdots,f^{k})
. For
f^{*}\in[-c/T,c/T]
, we can write the vector

\displaystyle{\cal V}_{k}(f^{*})=w_{1}{\cal V}_{k}(f_{1})+\dots+w_{k}{\cal V}_{k}(f_{k})

for some real numbers (depending on
f^{*}
)
w_{1},\dots,w_{k}
, because
{\cal V}_{k}(f_{1}),\cdots,{\cal V}_{k}(f_{k})
is linear independence. Thus,

\displaystyle g_{f^{*}}(t)=w_{1}g_{f_{1}}(t)+\dots+w_{k}g_{f_{k}}(t)

(103)

for the same weights. Now note that by (
B.5
), for all
t\in[0,T]
,

\displaystyle\lvert x^{*}(t)-v^{*}g_{f^{*}}(t)\rvert=

\displaystyle~\lvert v^{*}(g_{f^{*}}(t)-\exp(2\pi\mathbf{i}f^{*}t))\rvert

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~|v^{*}|\delta_{1}.

Then, because
g_{f}(t)
can be expressed by
g_{f_{j}}(t),j\in[k]
, we have that

\displaystyle\lvert x^{*}(t)-\sum_{j=0}^{k}v^{*}g_{f_{j}}(t)w_{j}\rvert=\lvert x^{*}(t)-v^{*}g_{f^{*}}(t)\rvert

which is follows from Eq. (
103
).

Note that

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{k}\lvert w_{j}\rvert\leq C_{1}

So by Eq. (
B.5
), we will transform the approximator of exponential function back to exponential function. For
\forall t\in[0,T]
,

\displaystyle\lvert\sum_{j=1}^{k}(g_{f_{j}}(t)-\exp(2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}t))(v^{*}w_{j})\rvert\leq C_{1}|v^{*}|\delta_{1}

Therefore, we can conclude that for
\forall t\in[0,T]

\displaystyle\lvert x^{*}(t)-\sum_{j=1}^{k}(v^{*}w_{j})\exp(2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}t)\rvert\leq C_{1}|v^{*}|\delta_{1}

and setting

\displaystyle h(x)=\sum_{j=1}^{k}(v^{*}w_{j})\exp(2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}t)

immediately leads to the desired conclusion.
∎

Lemma
B.11
immediately gives the following corollary by taking linear summation over
k
frequencies.

Corollary B.12
.

Let
0<\delta<0.1
be a parameter. Let
x^{*}
be any
k
-Fourier-sparse signal. Then, there exists
\widetilde{k}\lesssim k\log(k/\delta)
, universal constant
c\in(0,1)
,
f_{1},\cdots,f_{k}\in c/T\mathbb{Z}
, and
v_{1},\dots,v_{k}\in\mathbb{C}

and for the function

\displaystyle\widetilde{x}(t)=\sum_{j=1}^{\widetilde{k}}v_{j}\exp(2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}t)

we have that

\displaystyle\|x^{*}-\widetilde{x}\|_{T}\lesssim\delta\|\widehat{x}^{*}(f)\|_{1}

Appendix C
Improving Fourier Interpolation Precision in a Smaller Range

In this section, we show that the approximation error of the Fourier interpolation algorithm developed in Section
12
can be further improved, if we only care about the signal in a shorter time duration
[0,(1-c)T]
for
c\in(0,1)
. The main result of this section is Theorem
C.4
.

C.1
Control noise

Lemma C.1
.

Let
x^{*}(t)=\sum_{j=1}^{k}v_{j}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}t}
and
x(t)=x^{*}(t)+g(t)
be our observable signal. Let
\mathcal{N}_{1}^{2}:=\varepsilon_{1}(\|g(t)\|_{T}^{2}+\delta\|x^{*}(t)\|_{T}^{2})
. Let
C_{1},\cdots,C_{l}
are the
\mathcal{N}_{1}
-heavy clusters from Definition
12.11
. Let
S^{*}
denotes the set of frequencies
f^{*}\in\{f_{j}\}_{j\in[k]}
such that,
f^{*}\in C_{i}
for some
i\in[l]
, and

\displaystyle\int_{C_{i}}|\widehat{x^{*}\cdot H}(f)|^{2}\mathrm{d}f\geq T\mathcal{N}_{1}^{2}/k,

Let
S
denotes the set of frequencies
f^{*}\in S^{*}
such that,
f^{*}\in C_{j}
for some
j\in[l]
, and

\displaystyle\int_{C_{j}}|\widehat{x\cdot H}(f)|^{2}\mathrm{d}f\geq\varepsilon_{2}T\mathcal{N}_{1}^{2}/k,

Then, we have that,

\displaystyle\|x-x_{S}\|_{T^{\prime}}+\|x_{S}-x^{*}\|_{T^{\prime}}\leq(\sqrt{2}+O(\sqrt{\varepsilon}+c))\|g\|_{T}+O(\sqrt{\delta})\|x^{*}\|_{T}.

Proof.

Following from the fact that
\sqrt{1+\varepsilon}=1+O(\varepsilon)
for
\varepsilon<1
, we have

\mathcal{N}_{1}=\sqrt{\varepsilon_{1}(\|g\|_{T}^{2}+\delta\|x^{*}\|_{T}^{2})}\leq\sqrt{\varepsilon_{1}}\|g\|_{T}+\sqrt{\delta\varepsilon_{1}}\|x^{*}\|_{T}.

We have that

\displaystyle\|x-x_{S^{*}}\|_{T}\leq

\displaystyle~\|x-x^{*}\|_{T}+\|x^{*}-x_{S^{*}}\|_{T}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\|g\|_{T}+\|x^{*}-x_{S^{*}}\|_{T}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\|g\|_{T}+(1+\varepsilon)\mathcal{N}_{1},

(104)

where the first step follows from triangle inequality, the second step follows the definition of
g
, the third step follows from Claim
12.12
.

Therefore,

\displaystyle~\|x-x_{S}\|_{T^{\prime}}+\|x_{S}-x^{*}\|_{T^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\|x-x_{S}\|_{T^{\prime}}+\|x_{S}-x_{S^{*}}\|_{T^{\prime}}+\|x_{S^{*}}-x^{*}\|_{T^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\|x-x_{S}\|_{T^{\prime}}+\|x_{S}-x_{S^{*}}\|_{T^{\prime}}+(1+2c)\|x_{S^{*}}-x^{*}\|_{T^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~(1+2\delta)\|H(x-x_{S})\|_{T^{\prime}}+(1+2\delta)\|H(x_{S}-x_{S^{*}})\|_{T^{\prime}}+(1+2c)\|x_{S^{*}}-x^{*}\|_{T}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~(1+O(\delta))(1+2c)(\|H(x-x_{S})\|_{T}+\|H(x_{S}-x_{S^{*}})\|_{T})+(1+\varepsilon)(1+O(c))\mathcal{N}_{1}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~(1+O(\delta))(1+2c)\sqrt{2}\sqrt{\|H(x-x_{S})\|^{2}_{T}+\|H(x_{S}-x_{S^{*}})\|^{2}_{T}}+(1+\varepsilon)(1+O(c))\mathcal{N}_{1}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~(1+O(\delta))(1+O(\sqrt{\varepsilon_{2}}))(1+O(c))\sqrt{2}\|x-x_{S^{*}}\|_{T}+(1+\varepsilon)(1+O(c))\mathcal{N}_{1}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~(\sqrt{2}+O(\delta+\sqrt{\varepsilon_{2}}+c))(\|g\|_{T}+(1+\varepsilon)\mathcal{N}_{1})+(1+\varepsilon)(1+O(c))\mathcal{N}_{1}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~(\sqrt{2}+O(\sqrt{\varepsilon}+c))\|g\|_{T}+O(\sqrt{\delta})\|x^{*}\|_{T},

where the first step follows from triangle inequality, the second step follows from for any function
x:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
,
(1-c)\|x\|_{T^{\prime}}\leq\|x\|_{T}
, the third step follows from Property I of Lemma
12.7
and
(1-c)/2<(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{s_{1}})s_{3}
, the forth step follows from Claim
12.12
, the fifth step follows from
\|H(x-x_{S})\|_{T}+\|H(x_{S}-x_{S^{*}})\|_{T}\leq\sqrt{2}\sqrt{\|H(x-x_{S})\|_{T}^{2}+\|H(x_{S}-x_{S^{*}})\|_{T}^{2}}
, the sixth step follows from Lemma
12.21
, the seventh step follows from Eq. (
68
), the last step follows from
\varepsilon=\varepsilon_{0}=\varepsilon_{1}=\varepsilon_{2}
.
∎

Parameters setting

By Section C.3 in
[
CKPS16
]
, we choose parameters for filter function
(H(t),\widehat{H}(f))
as follows:

•

By Eq. (
12.2
) in the proof of Property VI of filter function
(H(t),\widehat{H}(f))
, we need
(1-s_{3}(1-\frac{2}{s_{1}}))\cdot\widetilde{O}(k^{4})\leq\varepsilon
, thus we have that
\min(\frac{1}{1-s_{3}},s_{1})\geq\widetilde{O}(k^{4})/\varepsilon
.

•

In the proof of Property V of filter function
(H(t),\widehat{H}(f))
, we set
\ell\gtrsim k\log(k/\delta)
.

•

In the proof of Lemma
C.1
, we set
(1-c)/2<(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{s_{1}})s_{3}
. Thus, we have that
\min(s_{3},1-\frac{4}{s_{1}})\geq 1-\frac{c}{2}
or equivalently
\min(\frac{1}{1-s_{3}},s_{1}/4)\geq\frac{2}{c}
.

•

\Delta_{h}
is determined by the parameters of filter
(H(t),\widehat{H}(f))
in Eq. (
39
):
\Delta_{h}\eqsim\frac{s_{1}\ell}{s_{3}T}
. Combining the setting of
s_{1}
,
s_{3}

\ell
, we should set
\Delta_{h}\geq\max(\widetilde{O}(k^{5}\log(1/\delta))/(\varepsilon T),O({k\log(k/\delta)}/{(cT)}))
.

C.2
(\sqrt{2}+\varepsilon)
-approximation ratio

Corollary C.2

(Corollary of Theorem
12.36
)
.

Let
x^{*}(t)={\sum_{j=1}^{k}}v_{j}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}t}
and
x(t)=x^{*}(t)+g(t)
be our observable signal where
\|g(t)\|_{T}^{2}\leq c_{0}\|x^{*}(t)\|_{T}^{2}
for a sufficiently small constant
c_{0}
. Then
Procedure
FrequencyRecoveryKCluster
returns a set
L
of
O(k/(\varepsilon_{0}\varepsilon_{1}\varepsilon_{2}))

frequencies that covers all
\mathcal{N}_{2}
-heavy clusters of
x^{*}
, which uses
\mathrm{poly}(k,c^{-1},\varepsilon^{-1},\varepsilon_{0}^{-1},\varepsilon_{1}^{-1},\varepsilon_{2}^{-1},\log(1/\delta))\log(FT)
samples and
\mathrm{poly}(k,c^{-1},\varepsilon^{-1},\varepsilon_{0}^{-1},\varepsilon_{1}^{-1},\varepsilon_{2}^{-1},\log(1/\delta))\log^{2}(FT)
time.

In particular, for
\Delta_{0}=c^{-1}\varepsilon^{-1}\mathrm{poly}(k,\log(1/\delta))/T
and
\mathcal{N}_{2}^{2}:=\varepsilon_{1}\varepsilon_{2}(\|g(t)\|_{T}^{2}+\delta\|x^{*}(t)\|_{T}^{2})
, with probability
1-2^{-\Omega(k)}
, for any
f^{*}
with

\int_{f^{*}-\Delta}^{f^{*}+\Delta}|\widehat{x\cdot H}(f)|^{2}\mathrm{d}f\geq T\mathcal{N}_{2}^{2}/k,

(105)

there exists an
\widetilde{f}\in L
satisfying

|f^{*}-\widetilde{f}|\lesssim\Delta_{0}\sqrt{\Delta_{0}T}.

Remark C.3
.

The proof is similar with the proof of Theorem
12.36
.

Theorem C.4

(
(\sqrt{2}+\varepsilon)
-approximate Fourier interpolation algorithm with shrinking range)
.

Let
x(t)=x^{*}(t)+g(t)
, where
x^{*}
is
k
-Fourier-sparse signal with frequencies in
[-F,F]
. Let
T^{\prime}=T(1-c)
. Given samples of
x
over
[0,T]
, we can output
y(t)
such that with probability at least
1-2^{-\Omega(k)}
,

\|y-x^{*}\|_{T^{\prime}}\leq(\sqrt{2}+\varepsilon+c)\|g\|_{T}+\delta\|x^{*}\|_{T}.

Our algorithm uses
\mathrm{poly}(k,\varepsilon^{-1},c^{-1},\log(1/\delta))\log(FT)

samples and
\mathrm{poly}(k,\varepsilon^{-1},c^{-1},\log(1/\delta))\cdot\log^{2}(FT)
time. The output
y

is
\mathrm{poly}(k,\varepsilon^{-1},c^{-1},\log(1/\delta))
-Fourier-sparse signal.

Remark C.5
.

[
LLM21
]
considers the following guarantee: for parameter
\delta>0
,
\|y(t)-x^{*}(t)\|_{(1-c)T}\leq\alpha\|g(t)\|_{T}+\delta\|\widehat{x}^{*}(f)\|_{1}
, where
\alpha
is the approximation ratio,
c
is the shrinking factor.
[
LLM21
]
uses
\mathrm{poly}(k,\log(1/\delta))\log(FT)
samples and
\mathrm{poly}(k,c^{-1},\log(1/\delta))\cdot\log^{2}(FT)
time, and recovers a
k\mathrm{poly}(1/c,\log(k/\delta))
-Fourier-sparse signal but only guarantee for
c\in(0,1)
and
\alpha=\mathrm{poly}(\log(k/(\delta c)))
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{N}_{1}^{2}:=\varepsilon_{1}(\|g(t)\|_{T}^{2}+\delta\|x^{*}(t)\|_{T}^{2})
be the heavy cluster parameter.

First, by Lemma
12.12
, there is a set of frequencies
S^{*}\subset[k]
and
x_{S^{*}}(t)=\underset{j\in S^{*}}{\sum}v_{j}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}t}
such that

\displaystyle\|x_{S^{*}}-x^{*}\|_{T}^{2}\leq(1+\varepsilon)\mathcal{N}_{1}^{2}.

(106)

Furthermore, each
f_{j}
with
j\in S
belongs to an
\mathcal{N}_{1}
-heavy cluster
C_{j}
with respect to the filter function
H
defined in Definition
12.6
.

By Definition
12.11
of heavy cluster, it holds that

\displaystyle\int_{C_{j}}|\widehat{H\cdot x^{*}}(f)|^{2}\mathrm{d}f\geq T\mathcal{N}_{1}^{2}/k.

By Definition
12.11
, we also have
|C_{j}|\leq k\cdot\Delta_{h}
, where
\Delta_{h}
is the bandwidth of
\widehat{H}
.

Let
\Delta\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, and
\Delta>k\cdot\Delta_{h}
, which implies that
C_{j}\subseteq[f_{j}-\Delta,f_{j}+\Delta]
. Thus, we have

\displaystyle\int_{f_{j}-\Delta}^{f_{j}+\Delta}|\widehat{H\cdot x^{*}}(f)|^{2}\mathrm{d}f\geq T\mathcal{N}_{1}^{2}/k.

By Corollary
12.22
, there is a set of frequencies
S\subset S^{*}
and
x_{S}(t)=\underset{j\in S}{\sum}v_{j}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}t}
such that

\displaystyle\|x_{S}-x_{S^{*}}\|_{T}^{2}\leq(1+O(\sqrt{\varepsilon_{2}}))\|x-x_{S^{*}}\|_{T}^{2}.

Let
g^{\prime}=x-x_{S^{*}}
.

Now it is enough to recover only
x_{S}
, instead of
x^{*}
.

By applying Theorem
C.2
, there is an algorithm that outputs a set of frequencies
L\subset\mathbb{R}
such that,
|L|=O(k/(\varepsilon_{0}\varepsilon_{1}\varepsilon_{2}))
, and with probability at least
1-2^{-\Omega(k)}
, for any
f_{j}
with
j\in S
, there is a
\widetilde{f}\in L
such that,

\displaystyle|f_{j}-\widetilde{f}|\lesssim\Delta\sqrt{\Delta T}.

We define a map
p:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow L
as follows:

\displaystyle p(f):=\arg\min_{\widetilde{f}\in L}~|f-\widetilde{f}|~~~\forall f\in\mathbb{R}.

Then,
x_{S}(t)
can be expressed as

\displaystyle x_{S}(t)=

\displaystyle~\sum_{j\in S}v_{j}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}t}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\sum_{j\in S}v_{j}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\cdot p(f_{j})t}\cdot e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\cdot(f_{j}-p(f_{j}))t}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\sum_{\widetilde{f}\in L}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\widetilde{f}t}\cdot\sum_{j\in S:~p(f_{j})=\widetilde{f}}v_{j}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}(f_{j}-\widetilde{f})t},

where the first step follows from the definition of
x_{S}(t)
, the last step follows from interchanging the summations.

For each
\widetilde{f}_{i}\in L
, by Corollary
12.2
with
x^{*}=x_{S},\Delta=\Delta\sqrt{\Delta T}
, we have that there exist degree
d=O(T\Delta\sqrt{\Delta T}+k^{3}\log k+k\log 1/\delta)
polynomials
P_{i}(t)
corresponding to
\widetilde{f}_{i}\in L
such that,

\displaystyle\|x_{S}(t)-\sum_{\widetilde{f}_{i}\in L}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\widetilde{f}_{i}t}P_{i}(t)\|_{T}\leq\delta\|x_{S}(t)\|_{T}

(107)

Define the following function family:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}:=\mathrm{span}\Big\{e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\widetilde{f}t}\cdot t^{j}~{|}~\forall\widetilde{f}\in L,j\in\{0,1,\dots,d\}\Big\}.

Note that
\sum_{\widetilde{f}_{i}\in L}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\widetilde{f}_{i}t}P_{i}(t)\in{\cal F}
.

By Claim
12.16
, for function family
\cal F
,
K_{\mathrm{Uniform[cT/2,T(1-c/2)]}}=O((|L|d)^{4}\log^{3}(|L|d))
.

By Lemma
12.18
, we have that, choosing a set
W
of
O(\varepsilon^{-1}K_{\mathrm{Uniform[cT/2,T(1-c/2)]}}\log(|L|d/\rho))

i.i.d. samples uniformly at random over duration
[0,T]
is a
(\varepsilon,\rho)
-WBSP.

By Lemma
12.19
, there is an algorithm that runs in
O(\varepsilon^{-1}|W|(|L|d)^{\omega-1}\log(1/\rho))
-time using samples in
W
, and outputs
y^{\prime}(t)\in{\cal F}
such that, with probability
1-\rho
,

\displaystyle\|y^{\prime}(t)-\sum_{\widetilde{f}_{i}\in L}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\widetilde{f}_{i}t}P_{i}(t)\|_{T^{\prime}}\leq(1+\varepsilon)\|x(t)-\sum_{\widetilde{f}_{i}\in L}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\widetilde{f}_{i}t}P_{i}(t)\|_{T^{\prime}}

(108)

Then by Lemma
12.3
, we have that there is a
O(kd)
-Fourier-sparse signal
y(t)
, such that

\displaystyle\|y(t)-y^{\prime}(t)\|_{T^{\prime}}\leq\delta^{\prime}

(109)

where
\delta^{\prime}>0
is any positive real number. Thus,
y
can be arbitrarily close to
y^{\prime}
.

Moreover, the sparsity of
y(t)
is
kd=kO(T\Delta\sqrt{\Delta T}+k^{3}\log k+k\log 1/\delta)=\mathrm{poly}(k,\varepsilon^{-1},c^{-1},\log(1/\delta))
.

Therefore, the total approximation error can be upper bounded as follows:

\displaystyle~\|y-x^{*}\|_{T^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\|y-y^{\prime}\|_{T^{\prime}}+\Big\|y-\sum_{\widetilde{f}_{i}\in L}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\widetilde{f}_{i}t}P_{i}(t)\Big\|_{T^{\prime}}+\Big\|\sum_{\widetilde{f}_{i}\in L}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\widetilde{f}_{i}t}P_{i}(t)-x^{*}\Big\|_{T^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~(1+0.1\varepsilon)\Big\|y-\sum_{\widetilde{f}_{i}\in L}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\widetilde{f}_{i}t}P_{i}(t)\Big\|_{T^{\prime}}+\Big\|\sum_{\widetilde{f}_{i}\in L}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\widetilde{f}_{i}t}P_{i}(t)-x^{*}\Big\|_{T^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~(1+2\varepsilon)\Big\|x-\sum_{\widetilde{f}_{i}\in L}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\widetilde{f}_{i}t}P_{i}(t)\Big\|_{T^{\prime}}+\Big\|\sum_{\widetilde{f}_{i}\in L}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\widetilde{f}_{i}t}P_{i}(t)-x^{*}\Big\|_{T^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~(1+2\varepsilon)(\Big\|x-x_{S}\Big\|_{T^{\prime}}+\Big\|x_{S}-x^{*}\Big\|_{T^{\prime}})+2(1+\varepsilon)\|x_{S}-\sum_{\widetilde{f}_{i}\in L}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\widetilde{f}_{i}t}P_{i}(t)\|_{T^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~(1+2\varepsilon)(\Big\|x-x_{S}\Big\|_{T^{\prime}}+\Big\|x_{S}-x^{*}\Big\|_{T^{\prime}})+O(\delta)\|x_{S}(t)\|_{T}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~(1+2\varepsilon)(\sqrt{2}+O(\sqrt{\varepsilon}+c))\|g\|_{T}+O(\sqrt{\delta})\|x^{*}\|_{T}+O(\delta)\|x_{S}(t)\|_{T}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~(1+2\varepsilon)(\sqrt{2}+O(\sqrt{\varepsilon}+c))\|g\|_{T}+O(\sqrt{\delta})\|x^{*}\|_{T}+O(\delta)(\|g\|_{T}+\|x^{*}\|_{T})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~(\sqrt{2}+O(\sqrt{\varepsilon}+c))\|g\|_{T}+O(\sqrt{\delta})\|x^{*}\|_{T},

where the first step follows from triangle inequality, the second step follows from Eq. (
109
), the third step follows from Eq. (
108
), the forth step follows from Triangle Inequality again, the fifth step follows from Eq. (
107
), the sixth step follows from Lemma
C.1
, the seventh step follows from Lemma
12.38
, and the last step is straightforward.

By re-scaling
\varepsilon
and
\delta
, we prove the theorem.

∎
